contributed to this year’s competition.

 tremendous e-orts, eight teams rose to the
highest distinction and were named 
Outstanding Winners. In addition, with the
support of the National Council of Teachers
of Mathematics (NCTM), two teams 
were honored with the NCTM Award.

COMAP remains commi+ed to presenting
engaging, real?world problems that challenge
and inspire students. As in previous 
years, teams selected from two distinct 
problems: Problem A, which focused on 
modeling Emergency Evacuation Sweeps, 
and Problem B, which explored the 
Environmental Impact of Sport Mega?Events.
The 2025 contest received 1091 submissions.
Of these, 585 addressed Problem A and
506 addressed Problem B. In total, 4023
students participated, representing a
wide range of schools and regions 
including teams from Australia, Brazil,
Canada, China, France, Germany, Hong
Kong (SAR) China, Japan, Macau (SAR)
China, Malaysia, Norway, Peru, Philip?
pines, Portugal, Singapore, South Africa,
South Korea, Taiwan China, Thailand,
Turkey, United Arab Emirates, United King?
dom, USA and Vietnam. Within the United
States, 230 teams represented 25 states.
Overview
For more than four decades of inter-
national modeling contests,  COMAP
has witnessed growing enthusiasm for
mathematical modeling. In 2025,
HiMCM marked its 28th year, as well as
the 25th anniversary of offering two prob-
lem choices. From an initial cohort of just
115 students, participation has expanded
and now over 55,000 students worldwide
have engaged with complex, meaningful
problems through HiMCM. The contest
welcomes all high school and below stu-
dents. Success is grounded not in advanced
coursework, but in a solid foundation of
high school mathematics and curiosity
about solving real-world problems.
We also wish to recognize the invaluable
role of advisors and teachers. These ded-
icated educators inspire students to extend
beyond the traditional curriculum and
embrace new ways of thinking. Their
mentorship is essential to the success and
continued growth of the HiMCM, and we
are deeply grateful for their commitment.
Rules and Expectations 
The core principle of the HiMCM is that
all work must be completed solely by
team members using inanimate sources
such as textbooks, research articles, and
other published materials. Collabo ?
ration outside the team, including con?
sultation with teachers, experts, or
external individuals, is not permi +ed.
Similarly, conducting surveys, inter ?
views,or acce ssing solutions shared
electronically by others violates contest
rules. Again, only the team members
may contribute to the solution through
their knowledge and work. Ad ?
ditionally, COMAP is committed to
ensuring that all necessary resources
for success are accessible without 
requiring additional purchases. The 
materials and information provided

by COMAP, along with a team’s 
knowledge, skills, and research, are 
all that is needed for success.  
The use of arti 2cial intelligence (AI) is
permi+ed in limited, transparent ways.
For example, AI may be used for 
research assistance, editing, or 
translation. All AI use must be fully
documented, including prompts and
outputs, in a report that does not
count toward the page limit.
Advisors and students o en ask what
level of mathematics is required and
what special coding skills are needed
for the contest. Our HiMCM problems
are accessible using high school 
level mathematics alone and no 
programming or coding skills are 
required.  As in all our contests, each of
our problems is accessible on multiple
levels, with many teams making use of
di-erent levels of mathematics and
technology tools. Students should
apply the mathematics they understand
and are able to explain in their solution
analysis, as the ability to clearly 
communicate their methods and 
rationale is valued in 2nal judging. 
COMAP understands that there is not
one standard or body of knowledge of
high school level mathematics for an 
international math modeling competition.
The judges see a wide range of abilities
and skills. Teams should note that using
lower levels of mathematics properly
can do just as well as higher levels of 
mathematics in the competition.  Teams
should not be focused on impressing
the judges with overly complex tech ?
niques as models but rather concentrate
on using good modeling tec hniques 
in putting together their solution.
COMAP uses X  and Weibo to provide
contest information to participants. Follow
us @COMAPMath on X or COMAPCHI?

ance and up to date contest information.
Judging Process
Judging is conducted blindly without
identifying information about teams or
schools. As open-ended problems, 
submissions are evaluated on their 
assumptions, reasoning, and modeling
approach rather than adherence to a
single correct answer with a rubric. All
submissions are reviewed electronically,
allowing for a diverse and highly qualified
judging panel to easily review. Each
paper is initially evaluated by at least
two preliminary judges. We extend our
sincere thanks to these judges for their
thoughtful and thorough assessments.
After the preliminary round, papers are
categorized as Finalist, Meritorious, 
Honorable Mention, or Successful Partic-
ipant. Finalist papers advance to a final
round of judging, where the most excep-
tional submissions are selected as Out-
standing. This year, 105 papers advanced
to final judging, with eight ultimately
earning the Outstanding designation.
Looking Ahead
The HiMCM continues to evolve as a
platform for students to apply math-
ematics in meaningful and collabo-
rative ways. Additionally, COMAP
has expanded opportunities through
the MidMCM, now in its fifth year,
offering middle school/level age 
students the chance to engage in
mathematical modeling. Both contests
emphasize the power of mathematics as a
tool for understanding and representing
the world. Students develop critical 
thinking, communication, and teamwork
skills while gaining confidence in tackling 
complex, open-ended challenges. Impor-
tantly, no advanced mathematics or 
programming experience is required.
 Success lies in clear reasoning, effective
communication, and thoughtful applica-
tion of accessible mathematical tools.
Judges value clarity and sound method-
ology over unnecessary complexity.
We encourage advisors to continue 
fostering creativity, curiosity, and 
perseverance in their students. COMAP
encourages all mathematics faculty to
get involved, motivate your students to
be problem solvers, make mathematics
relevant, and open the doors to future
success.  We want to partner with
teachers as we continually strive to 
improve the contest and make it accessible
and impactful to all students. Together,
we can ensure that mathematical 
modeling remains an engaging, acces-
sible, and impactful experience for all. 
2026 Contest Dates
Mark your calendars for the 6th annual
MidMCM, and the 29th HiMCM, to be
held November 4 ? 17, 2026. Registration
for the 2026 MidMCM and HiMCM will
open in September. Student teams may
work at any time during the contest 
window. At the team members’ conven?
ience, teams download and choose their
problem, complete their modeling 
solution, and electronically submit their
solution document by the deadline on 
November 17th. Again in 2026, one team
for each problem will receive the NCTM
award. Teams can learn more about
COMAP’s contests and registration at
www.comap.org/.

and judges’ commentaries that can 
provide guidance for teams preparing

The following guidance re ects the 
qualities our judges consistently value in
HiMCM papers. Strong submissions 
present a clear and comprehensive
summary and address all aspects of the
problem through the thoughtful 
development and application of one or
more mathematical models. The most
competitive papers distinguish themselves
through clarity, organization, and well?sup?
ported reasoning, demonstrating both care?
ful analysis and e-ective communication.
The strongest papers go beyond meeting
the basic requirements. They present
complete and well ?integrated math ?
ematical and logical analysis in a 
cohesive and engaging manner. These
submissions are not only thorough but
also accessible.  They are easy to follow,
logically structured, and oen creative in
their approach. They include clearly stated
assumptions with appropriate justi2ca?
tions, a transparent description of the
modeling process, well?explained results
and analysis, and thoughtful discussion
of strengths and limitations, including 
sensitivity analysis. Strong conclusions
and appropriate references further 
support the overall quality of the work.
Our judges continue to emphasize that
HiMCM problems are designed to be
accessible using high school?level math?
ematics. While some teams a +empt to
incorporate advanced concepts or tools,
these approaches are only e-ective when
they are clearly explained and 
appropriately applied. Papers that rely
on techniques beyond a team’s ability to
fully justify or interpret are oen less suc?
cessful. We are not seeking the use of the
most advanced mathematics. Rather, the
most successful papers are those in which
teams develop models grounded in
mathematical ideas they understand, can
clearly explain, and can apply e-ectively.

Problem A: Emergency
Evacuation Sweeps
For the 2rst time 25 years ago, COMAP
posed two problems for High School
students to choose from during the
HIMCM.  To honor this anniversary, we
opted to revisit Problem A but with a
di-erent scenario.  In 2000, student
teams were asked to develop an ecient
algorithm for police cars to sweep the
area to force bank robbers (who were
eeing by car) into one of the established
roadblocks.  Today, we challenge teams
to conduct a di -erent type of sweep.  
During emergencies such as 2res or gas
leaks, rapid and orderly evacuation of a
building is critical for saving lives.
Trained personnel responding to these
emergencies (responders) o en sweep
a building – going from area to area and
room to room – to ensure all occupants
have evacuated safely or are actively
moving towards the exits. This would
include visually assessing, if possible,
all spaces to con2rm that they are empty
with the goal of guaranteeing no one is
le behind. The eciency and strategy
behind these sweeps could mean the dif?
ference between safety and true peril. 
Teams were asked by the Clearing Of
Multi?use Assembly Public/Private ?
spaces (COMAP) with designing a
mathematical model to optimize sweep?
ing strategies in multi ?oor buildings
during emergency evacuations. The goal
is to ensure all rooms are cleared in 
the shortest possible time, while priori?
tizing occupant safety and responder 
eciency. Buildings vary in layout,
number of oors, stairwell placement,
location of exits, and occupancy pa+erns
while responders face pressure to act
quickly with sometimes limited 
information. These considerations
must be included in your analysis. 
This problem makes teams look at 
emergency evacuation from di-erent per?
spectives. Students have been taking part
in 2re drills in school throughout their
lives; now they can see it from the other
perspective while considering both the

Problem B: Environmental
Impact of Sport Mega-
Events
Every year, the Super Bowl brings 
together millions of people – from fans
traveling to the host city, to viewers 
tuning in from around the globe. This 
event generates enormous social and 
economic value, but it also comes with a
significant environmental cost. With air
travel, hotel stays, energy use, food 
consumption, waste generation, and mas-
sive broadcast operations, the Super Bowl’s
environmental footprint is substantial. 
As we grow more concerned about 
sustainability, large-scale sporting events
or sport mega-events like the Super Bowl
are being made more environmentally
responsible. External organizations have
developed models and scoring systems
to rate the sustainability of these large-
scale events based on many factors from
energy use and waste management to
transportation and carbon offsets. 
While these environmental scores exist
and a wealth of data is available on what
event planners are doing to be

ere we will examine in-
depth what judges were
looking for in each aspect of
a well-written submission.  For each

Overall
Student participants are expected to 
ensure that their papers adhere to all
contest rules as outlined on the o cial
contest website. Submissions must be
presented within the 25?page limit and
use a minimum 12?point font. Each year,
judges encounter papers that a+empt to
exceed these limits by reducing font size,
adjusting margins, or adding additional
pages. While such submissions are not
automatically disquali 2ed, they are 
unlikely to earn top designations.
Papers that advance to the Finalist and
Outstanding levels create a positive and
lasting impression and consistently 
demonstrate a high degree of clarity, 
organization and coherence. They begin
with a clear and informative summary
and provide a structured preview of the
paper. These submissions present 
well?justi2ed assumptions, clearly 
explain the development and application
of their model, and support their results
with appropriate mathematical analysis.
The strongest papers also include 
sensitivity analysis, discuss model
strengths and limitations, and conclude
with clear, well ?supported 2ndings.
Ultimately, it is not the length of a paper
but the clarity, completeness, and 
e-ectiveness of its communication that
distinguishes strong submissions.
Before submission, teams are encouraged
to carefully review their work, including
the use of spelling and grammar tools. For
international teams, all symbols and labels
in tables and 2gures should be presented
in English. To ensure fairness in the 
judging process, student and school names
must not appear anywhere in the paper.
Executive Summary
The executive summary is an essential
component of a standout modeling
paper as it requires synthesizing the
most critical information from the report
into a clear, concise overview of the work
to follow. A memorable executive 
summary sets the tone for what can be
expected throughout the report, while
also being able to stand alone as a 
complete depiction of the problem from
its introduction to its conclusion includ-
ing implementable recommendations.
Teams are encouraged to write the Execu-
tive Summary after completing their paper
so they can accurately capture the entire
analysis. Once written, it should be re-
viewed carefully to ensure it stands alone
as a representation of the full submission.
For Problem A, Team 16486’s executive
summary (Example 1) found an effective
mix between providing high-level, lay-
person overview of their model while
including technical keywords to prime
the reader as to what the implementation
would entail. They include numbers
quantifying the performance of 
their model that are understood in the
context of the problem statement and
finish their summary with a concise de-
scription of their key contributions.
For problem B, this meant addressing
both the Super Bowl and another global
mega-event and addressing the key 
environmental impact factors chosen to
develop the model around. An excellent
example of this is from Team #17173 
(Example 2). Despite being one of the
shortest executive summaries submitted,
it efficiently and clearly summarized the
goals, methods and results of their work
in a way that was distinct from the main
body of the report. This summary whets
the appetite of the reader, enticing them
to read further to learn more about how
the locations for the events were chosen.
Demonstrated Contextual
Understanding 
Strong papers demonstrate a clear 
understanding of the complexity of the
problem beyond what is explicitly stated
in the prompt. This understanding is
oen reected in the introduction, where
the be +er teams move beyond simply
restating the problem to provide their
own unique meaningful insight into the
bigger picture. Some teams incorporate
background research or elements of a 
literature?style review, though this is not
required.  Teams should know that what
ma+ers most is the depth of understand?
ing conveyed.  This deeper perspective
strengthens the entire paper. It enables
teams to more clearly de2ne the problem
and make thoughtful modeling decisions.
Teams are encouraged to make the 
introduction their own by establishing
a strong foundation before beginning
their model development. Judges

between types of sport mega?events in a
section entitled “Comparative Analysis of
Model Results” which truly demonstrated
to judges that they understood why they
were conducting the analysis they had.
This section was not a problem require?
ment but instead demonstrated this team’s
deeper understanding of the bigger picture
problem which was to determine what 
factors ma+er the most for di-erent types
of sport mega?events when it comes to de?
termining the most sustainable host cities.  
Assumptions
Teams are expected to clearly state all
assumptions, either as a dedicated list
or within the context of the paper where
they are applied. Assumptions play a
critical role in simplifying complex real?world
problems and addressing gaps in data or
knowledge. E-ective assumptions are pur?
poseful and directly connected to the model.
Each assumption should be clearly justi2ed,
with reasoning that explains both why it is
necessary and how it supports the modeling
approach. Judges look for assumptions that
are thoughtfully chosen and meaningfully
integrated into the analysis, rather than 
included as general statements.
Teams are encouraged to consider where and
how each assumption is used within their
model. The strongest papers not only state
and justify their assumptions but also dem?
onstrate explicitly how those assumptions
inuence the development, application,
and interpretation of the model.
Judges for Problem A looked for teams
that used assumptions as a tool to assist
in building the base model for searches
to eliminate complexities so the base
model could be built.  Those teams
would then build further models without
the assumption constraints of the base
model.  For example, in the paper
wri+en by Team 16486 (Example 7), the
team clearly states an assumption that
will be in place for the base model.  The
team later builds upon the base model
when assumption 5 is no longer in place.  
Additionally, judges looked for papers
that referenced where they obtained their
data for certain assumptions. The judges
appreciated when teams clearly referenced
where they obtained the speed they used
for the movement of 2re2ghters like in
Team 16691 s paper (Example 8).
In Problem B, the paper wri+en by Team
17205 (Example 9) was very readable,
but they did an exceptional job of 
making their assumptions clear by 
organizing them into categories.
Model Selection
There are many valid approaches to 
modeling HiMCM problems, and judges
appreciate appropriate and creative 
solutions. The most successful teams
identify the key factors driving their
model and explain the reasoning behind
their chosen approach. This includes 
discussion of model inputs, structure, and
data, as well as any adaptations made
when using existing models. Strong papers
often acknowledge the distinction between
an ideal model and what is feasible given
available data and time constraints.
While teams may explore multiple 
approaches, successful papers present
a clear and focused modeling strategy.
Rather than introducing several 
disconnected models, strong submissions 
develop a primary model that is refined
or extended as needed. Judges consistently
favor well-reasoned, clearly applied
models over overly complex approaches
that are difficult to justify or implement.
While the problem statement for 
Problem A did not make direct mention
of graphs or networks, almost every
team used a network model as the 
underlying framework. How these
 CONSORTIUM 130 Spring/Summer                          
HiMCM Contest
CM C

77
graphs were adapted to finding optimal
paths varied among the submissions
though, with some papers using a 
linear programming formulation, some
papers using optimal graph path 
algorithms like A*, traveling salesman,
or Dijkstra, and/or some papers incor-
porating stochastic simulations to 
explore these relatively large state spaces.
One common approach was to formulate
room allocation and path planning in
one swoop using a linear program or
similar optimization framework. Team
16486 (Example 10) used this approach,
effectively describing their set-up in a
direct manner. They formulated the
sweep-plan as a room and edge assign-
ment problem and described several
constraints their solution should meet,
which encoded that a sweep-plan must
travel along consecutive edges and every
room must be swept, among others. With
these determined, they then naturally
define their objective function, which 
allocates all room assignments and 
responder paths in one swoop. In their
model evaluation, they also considered
three other strategies for room assignment,
showing that some thought went into
deciding to use a linear program.
In slight contrast, many teams opted to
build and describe their models in a way
that highlighted properties of the 
networks a little more explicitly. This is
not to say one approach is better than
another, but rather the tools that become
applicable are more easily seen in one
versus another. This is the approach
Team 17401 (Example 11) used, splitting
the room allocation and path-finding
problems into two pieces, and using
graph algorithms for the second piece.
In their model, rooms of a building were
first clustered based on expected 
workload to clear them, after which an
optimal path was computed using a 
traveling salesman problem solver they
built and adapted to this problem.
Team 16603 (Example 12) used a similar
approach as above, splitting the problem
into a room allocation and then path-finding
problem, though in this case they used
existing traveling salesman solvers for the
initial path construction. The networks
they constructed were dynamic, so it
could happen that a responder’s original
path is no longer available; in these 
instances, their model used an A* algo-
rithm to find a way around these 
obstacles. Moreover, they effectively used
Monte Carlo simulations for the dynamic
graphs to gather data and provide strong 
evidence for the optimality of sweep-plans
they construct in the presence of noise.
These last two models naturally followed
from their approach to the problem: first
identify who should search which
rooms, then figure out how to get them
there. This contrasts with the first model
mentioned, in which the two steps are per-
formed at the same time. As stated earlier,
neither approach is better than another -
teams should explore the problem and 
potential solutions and let their work tell
them which approach makes the most sense.
For Problem B, there were many modeling
options and techniques available to teams
participating in this competition, although
many teams utilized similar approaches.
What set teams apart was their ability to
not only describe what they did, but why
they used the approaches they chose. For
example, many groups this year used
Analytical Hierarchy Process (AHP) and
Entropy Weight Method (EWM) together
for a multi-criteria decision-making
model and justified balancing the sub-
jectivity of one with the inherent objec-
tivity of the other. The main factor dis-
tinguishing groups using similar
approaches was the ability to justify how
the rankings were determined, or how
the balance between methods was de-
termined for their multi-criteria decision-
making model. There were some excel-
lent papers submitted that did not use
the most powerful or unique modeling
techniques but distinguished themselves
by clearly and effectively explaining the
reasoning behind their choices.  For 
examples, judges chose to present the
three Outstanding papers as they all had
more standard modeling approaches with
excellent extensions and explanations. 
For their multi-criteria decision-making
model, the Outstanding Team 16442
used AHP and EWM to determine the
weights of their indices.  This team used
five core indices of environmental impact
based on over 14 contributing factors.
This allowed them to create their City
Impact Evaluation Model (CIEM) to
evaluate potential host cities for the
Super Bowl followed using Technique
for Order of Preference by Similarity to
Ideal Solution (TOPSIS) for the 
determination of a sustainability score
which can then be ranked.  What judges
appreciated the most about this standard 
approach was the team’s explanations of
the process and their adaptation of this
method for their model extension (Example
13).  When Team 16442 went to determine
the best potential host countries for the
World Cup, they adapted their initial
CIEM by refining the five core indices and
then added one more index to cover the
environmental impact of infrastructure.  
The Outstanding Team 16738 chose a
similar methodology for their multi-
criteria decision-making model.  They
utilized 12 secondary indicators and then
Analytic Network Process (ANP) with
EWM followed by TOPSIS.  The team
explained their choice of ANP over AHP
(Example 14) because they wanted to
capture the interdependencies between
criteria.  The judges appreciated the
analysis that followed which allowed the
team to extrapolate into the future based
on what they determined would be the
two indices that would most likely
change significantly over a five-year
period due to newly established public
policy (Example 15).  It was insightful
for the team to note and understand that
a sustainability score for 2029 might be
significantly different than today’s score
if certain city’s trends in implementing
more green practices were considered.

Finally, the Outstanding Team 16739 
approached their model creation a bit
differently, still using techniques that are
regularly seen by judges throughout this

but then took it further based on Pearson

used Autoregressive Integrated Moving
Average (ARIMA) models to forecast 
future city characteristics for both cities
that have posted and three that have not
yet hosted.  The identified features were
then inputted back into the original 
analysis before conducting EWM.
Model Implementation
Teams must apply their model to generate
meaningful results and demonstrate how
those results address the goals of the 
problem. This requires more than presenting
outputs as teams should interpret their 
2ndings in the context of the model’s 
purpose and explain how the results inform
the original questions.  Strong papers reect
thoughtfully on their results, considering
whether the model e-ectively captures the
key aspects of the problem and whether the
outcomes are reasonable and actionable. 
Once the theoretical development of their
model is developed, teams are expected to
apply their work to the problem to get tan?
gible results that address the problem
statement. In Problem A’s case, teams
were expected to implement their models
to analyze response times for a simple
six room o ce building and then 
consider at least two more building layouts.
Many teams de2ned a dynamic weighted
graph representing the geometry and 
logistics for sweeping a building, allowing
for dynamic hazards and the subsequent
e-ects on the network and 2rst responders’
possible sweep ?plans. Team 16843’s 
implementation (Example 17) stood out
to the judges for the in?depth and explicit
implementation framework for their 
optimal paths, which took shape as 2ve 
algorithms wri+en in pseudocode accom?
panying their discussion. This helped bridge
the gap between theory and implementation,
which made analysis of t heir model’s 
results easier to digest and understand. 
Additionally, Problem A judges enjoyed the
creativity displayed by teams in the variety
of examples considered with their two 
additional buildings, especially when teams
used building layouts based on buildings
relevant to their lives. This was one of the
reasons Team 17402 stood out during 2nal
judging: they considered actual layouts for
a nearby kindergarten school (Example 18),
as well as a oor of a high school building
(Example 19), and performed an extensive
analysis of their model applied to each 
(resulting in an optimal number of 3 and 4
2re2ghters to sweep the kindergarten 
and high school oor respectively).
In Problem B, the 2rst application was
to 2nding the most sustainable host city
for the Super Bowl.  Many teams simply
took their model and ran it to determine
the best host city for 2029.  Judges
wanted to highlight two teams that went
further on their implementation by 
providing a di-erentiation and expansion
that made their analysis more trustworthy
and demonstrated to judges that they
understood the details of this bigger
problem.  Team 16947 (Example 20) 
understood the data di-erences between
teams that have previously hosted the
Super Bowl and teams that have never
hosted.  This creates di culties in the
model when a +empting to determine
who should host in the future.  This team
implemented them separately and then
nicely brought the lists together for their
results discussion.  Judges also appreciate
the work that Team 17173 (Example 21)
conducted to add time adjustments.
They recognized that cities hosting in 
earlier years might obtain lower scores
using their model because of technological
limitations.  As they did not want the
temporal e -ect to penalize host cities
from the earlier Super Bowl years, they
implemented this time adjustment
methods aer the initial model results
and then compared the di-erences.  They
then appropriately made their deter ?
mination based on the updated model. 
Model Cohesion & 
Integration
Teams should guide the reader logically
from assumptions to model development,
application, and results, ensuring that
each step is connected. Papers that 
demonstrate this coherence are the most
e-ective. While teams may analyze 
multiple aspects of a problem, the greatest
value comes from integrating these 
components into a cohesive narrative that
addresses the overall question. Strong
papers demonstrate how the require ?
ments connect, whether by building upon
an initial model or adapting it to address
additional dimensions of the problem.
When multiple models or approaches are
used, teams should clearly justify these
choices and explain how each contributes
to a deeper understanding of the prob?
lem. The most successful papers move
beyond isolated analyses to present a
connected line of reasoning that adds
meaning and insight to the bigger picture
problem teams are trying to solve.
In Problem A, teams overwhelmingly
adapted graph ?based approaches to
build their model and apply it to the 
scenarios given in the problem statement.
Judges particularly appreciated the 
discussion in Team 16390s development
(Example 22), wherein they built their
model while including pseudocode and
enough details that it was clear how the
model was to be applied, and how their
unique approach to task allocation was to
be integrated in their graph search and
sweep model. This was followed by a brief
model validation, then presentation of 
results, and 2nally a discussion of sensi?
tivity. They presented a cohesive model 
discussion (from development to results) in
this section, which gave judges a clear view
of their approach for a single level building
before extending their model to multiple
oors, and other more complex scenarios.

model and then continually re2ning it to
add the complexities that a team wishes
to analyze is highly sought aer by judges.  
Sensitivity Analysis
Sensitivity analysis is an important com-
ponent of the modeling process, as it 
examines how changes in assumptions,
parameters, or input data affect the 
results of a model. It helps determine the
robustness, reliability, and overall 
usefulness of the model. Effective sensi-
tivity analysis often includes revisiting
simplifying assumptions and evaluating
how variations in key inputs influence
outcomes. While there is no single 
required method, judges look for clear
rationale behind the approach taken and
thoughtful interpretation of the results.
Strong papers use sensitivity analysis to
justify the level of confidence in their
model and its conclusions. While some
teams present this analysis as a dedicated
section, those that integrate it throughout
their work, connecting it directly to
model development and implementation
stand out. Judges feel that a well-ex-
ecuted sensitivity analysis demonstrates
a deeper understanding of the model’s
behavior and its practical implications.
Solutions to this year’s Problem A, Emer-
gency Evacuation Sweeps, included a
variety of factors and objectives. In 
reflecting on the stability of their models,
most teams needed to consider the 
relative importance of a number of 
parameters describing the effects of such
things as the walking speed of 
responders, changes due to the presence
of smoke or clutter, or the number
and/or condition of possible victims in
the building. In general, such explorations
involve systematic variation of parameter
values so that the influence of that 
parameter may be isolated. Ideally, such 
influences would then be compared and
interpreted in the context of the overall
objective of the emergency sweep model. 
Team 17460’s sensitivity analysis discussion
(Example 24) of its process is particularly
strong. They describe exactly how the 
analysis was conducted, identifying the 
parameters to be tested, the range of values
to be used, the method of testing, and the
key output of interest, all clearly enough
that the reader could duplicate their work
independently. They report their results in
easy-to-read, easy-to-compare graphical
form, and the final discussion provides
practical implications of these comparisons
for planners for emergency response teams.
Judges from Problem B were looking for an
extensive sensitivity analysis as there were
many assumptions teams should make, data
that had to be researched and incorporated,
as well as a variety of parameters for most
models.  The presentation of the sensitivity
analysis by Team 16442 (Example 25) was
notable for its depth followed by the 
explanations of the meaning.  The team 
conducted two examinations, and each was
followed by graphics with results and then
an analysis of results.  Additionally, judges
were impressed that the team brought it all 
together with a conclusion that reinforced
their confidence in their selection for the
most environmentally sustainable choice 
for the Super Bowl host city in 2029.
Strengths, Limitations and
Conclusions
After completing the modeling and 
analysis, teams should include a clear
discussion of the strengths and limitations
of their model, along with concluding
insights. This process pushes students to
step back and look at the bigger picture
of what their model can achieve and where
there may be room for improvements,
which is entirely reasonable and 
expected given the time constraints of
the contest. It is easy to fall into the trap
of saying “Our model is amazing and can
solve any problem!” but judges are looking
for a realistic assessment. Effective 
discussions highlight what the model
does well by identifying the types of
questions it is best suited to address
while also acknowledging its limitations.
These limitations may stem from 
simplifying assumptions, data constraints,
or modeling choices. Strong papers use
this section to reflect on the entire 
modeling process, offering insight into
what worked well, where improvements
could be made, and how the model
might be extended with additional time
or data. In many cases, judges are already
familiar with the strengths and limitations
of a particular modeling approach, and 
it is refreshing to see teams arrive at 
these same conclusions independently.
Clear, well-supported concluding thoughts
that tie back to the problem and results are
essential, ensuring the paper ends with a
sense of purpose. With so many demands
asked for in a modeling problem like this, 
it is easy to overlook the importance of 
including a conclusion in the report.  The
conclusion should contain direct recommen-
dations as we solve these real-world prob-
lems to provide insight to interested parties. 
Team 16691 in Problem A (Example 26) did
 CONSORTIUM 130 Spring/Summer                                  
HiMCM Contest
80
an excellent job highlighting the capabilities
of their model as it represented real safety
and rescue priorities. They also chose 
robust optimization tools. This team also
gave an honest reflection by relating their
limitations directly back to specific assump-
tions. The judges also appreciate that their
strengths and weaknesses were not presented
as a bullet list but written out clearly as a
cohesive discussion. It gave the impression
that the students really took time to justify
each of their assumptions and saw the 
connection to their model’s value.
In Problem B, Team 16624 (Example 27)
recognized that the environmental 
performance of a city does not remain
stagnant from 2023 to 2029, as assumed in
their model. The team acknowledged that
future environmental performance could
be forecast by incorporating data from 2023
and earlier. They identified the inclusion
of planned sustainability upgrades for a
city as a direction for future work.
Non-Technical Document
The purpose of a le +er or other non ?
technical document is to communicate
model?based recommendations in a way
that is accessible to a non ?technical 
audience. This section demonstrates a
team’s ability to translate complex 
mathematical analysis into practical, 
understandable insights. Since the 
intended audience oen lacks technical ex?
pertise, teams must present their 2ndings
in clear, concise language, using an 
appropriate format such as a le+er, memo, 
infographic, or article, as speci2ed in the
prompt. Rather than simply stating general
practices, strong submissions explain 
what the model reveals about the 
implications of a particular course of action.
While some teams may think of it as a
secondary component, the non?technical
document can be a distinguishing feature
of a strong paper. The most e-ective sub?
missions reect a clear understanding of
the modeling results and communicate
them in a way that is both informative
and actionable for decision ?makers.
Judges thought that Team 16846 
e-ectively conveyed their approach to 
non?technical audiences for Problem A 
(Example 28), which was ampli 2ed by
the team publishing the le+er at the start
of their paper right aer their executive
summary. They provided a brief 
overview of their approach before spend?
ing the most space discussing their results
in context, including some diagrams of
actual plans produced by their model.
For Problem B, judges really focused on
the omission of specialized mathematical
terminology and the avoidance of 
acronyms without clear de 2nitions. 
Because the le +er is addressed to 
executives in the NFL rather than to 
students or instructors in mathematical
modeling, accessibility and simplicity
were essential. Many papers met or 
exceeded this standard of pro 2ciency.
However, Team #16739 (Example 29)
provided one of the strongest templates
for a non?technical le+er. The le+er began
with a concise summary of the model
considerations, followed by the rec ?
ognition of the winning city. To persuade
the NFL of their recommendation, the team
explained the reasons for their choice in
clear, common language rather than
technical detail. The le+er concluded by
identifying other potential host cities and
o-ering an inspiring rationale for mak?
ing environmental impact an important
factor in the selection of a host city.
Appropriate Explanations
Discussions throughout the paper
should be clear and detailed to allow the
reader to follow the team’s modeling
process from beginning to end. This 
includes not only describing what was
done, but also explaining why choices
were made, with connections between
modeling decisions and the requirements
of the problem. Every year, judges seek
to highlight submissions that o -er a 
balance of sound mathematics with well
wri+en justi2cations of their approach.
Judges looked for implementable 
measures that were backed by 
research and then well explained.  
The Emergency Evacuation Sweeps
problem of Problem A is closely related
to the well ?known Traveling Salesman
Problem of graph theory. Essentially all
student solutions to Problem A began
with converting a oor plan and
frequently other important information
into a vertex?edge graphical representation.
Starting with such a representation the
task then becomes two ?fold: (i) 
generate a set of paths that covers all
rooms, and (ii) minimize an appropriate
“cost” function associated with such
paths. While it is possible to formulate a
“brute force” solution to these tasks, such
an approach becomes computationally 
impractical very quickly. The examples
below illustrate (portions of) two di-erent 
approaches to addressing this diculty. In
each case the teams made it easy for the
reader to follow their work, using clear 
language to explain and provide essential
information such as values of key parameters
and references for further information.
Team 17401 (Example 30) addressed the
computational di culty by breaking
their task into two steps, 2rst partitioning
rooms into localized groups, then 
building appropriate paths. For each task,
the students provided clear instructions
on exactly how the work is carried out.
In addition to these instructions, they
provided citations for each step of the
process, making it possible for the reader
to carry out their work independently
but also to 2nd additional theoretical 
discussion of the methods used.
Team 16691’s approach (Example 31) to
solving the problem of computational com?
plexity uses Ant Colony Optimization, a
probabilistic simulation. Even though this
approach may not be as familiar as more
direct methods, the students provided first
a general overview of the ideas behind the
method, then gave the details of their actual
set?up, including their selected parameter
values and stopping rule. The reader would
easily “follow along” with the solution pro?
cess. Again, as is the case in Team 17401’s
solution, reference to the method is cited.
For Problem B, judges believe that what
set teams apart was their ability to not

only describe what they did in their
modeling process, but why they used
the approaches they chose. There were
some excellent papers submi+ed that did
not use the most powerful or unique

explaining the reasoning behind their

#16738 (Example 32) did an excellent job of
highlighting the disparity between potential

for their inclusion in their model.
Organization & Writing
Strong papers are well?structured, clearly
wri+en, and easy to follow, guiding the reader
smoothly through the team’s approach and
analysis. A clear organizational framework 
allows the reader to understand how each
component of the paper contributes to the
overall solution. E -ective submissions 
consistently connect assumptions, methods,
and results back to the context of the
problem. The strongest papers enhance
understanding through thoughtful presen?
tation. Equations are paired with clear 
explanations, and visual elements such as
graphs or tables are used when appropriate
to communicate complex ideas. Overall,
strong communication demonstrates a 
deliberate e-ort to make the modeling 
process accessible to the reader.
Team # 16603 (Example 33) submi +ed
a well ?organized and well ?wri+en
paper for their solution to Problem A.
Note that even though it was outlined
similar to the template, it had a natural
ow between sections and information
was presented in a reasonable order.
One judge in particular appreciated the
way that the de2nitions of the variables
were well?organized with descriptions
of why and how they were used with
units included. When they presented a
relationship between variables or a piece
of their model, they explained why it
made sense. They also summarized all
of their 2ndings together and included
sections on validating their model.
In Problem B, papers that impressed the
judges had clear explanations of the models.
Judges valued knowing why choices were
made. Explaining abbreviations is helpful
to the reader. When diagrams or graphs
were used, the explanations of them
should immediately precede or follow
the graph or diagram. Judges appreciated
papers that had been proofread and
were free of spelling errors or incomplete
sentences. We recognize that teams used
AI platforms to help with translation and
clarity, but teams should make sure their
2nal papers use authentic language. Team
#16442 (Example 34) wrote a very read?
able and organized paper throughout.
Tables & Figures
Effective visualizations, such as charts,
graphs, and diagrams, play an important
role in enhancing understanding and
supporting key points within a report.
When used thoughtfully, they can 
clarify complex relationships and make 
results more accessible to the reader.
However, visualizations should not stand
alone. Each must be clearly explained
within the body of the paper, with explicit
discussion of what it shows, why it is 
included, and how it supports the team’s
analysis. Tables and figures should 
complement the written analysis rather
than replace it, helping to communicate
complex ideas more clearly and reinforcing
the team’s reas oning and findings.
For Problem A, the paper written by Team
16486 had so many exemplars for figures.
The floor plan conversions in Figures 3,
5, and 7 (Example 35) were clear, showing
side-by-side comparisons of architectural
layouts and their corresponding node-
graph representations with explicit node
labels that make the abstraction process
transparent and reproducible. Figure 6
and Figure 8 (Example 36) which were
the optimal sweep routes for the school
and house layouts, made effective use
of color-coding, assigning distinct colors
to each responder team's route, clarifying
how the optimizer naturally partitions
buildings into intuitive zones. Table 3
and Table 4 (Example 37) communicated
the four-factor priority system effectively
by showing both the raw hazard ratings
and the resulting normalized weights,
allowing the reader to understand why
certain rooms received priority over
others. Figure 9's (Example 38) dual 
presentation bar charts compared four
strategies and line graphs showing
sweep time versus responder count, 
provided compelling visual evidence of
the MILP model superiority over 
random, greedy, and manual-split 
approaches. Furthermore, all numerical
charts had clear and readable axis labels.
For Problem B, there were so many teams
that presented excellent tables and figures
with descriptive labels and explanations
embedded in the accompanying section’s
text.  Judges wanted to highlight the way
Team #16517 used (Example 39) 
tables to summarize information. For 
example, Table 20 conveys the comparison
of the important factors between hosting
a Superbowl and the Olympics.
Creativity
Creativity is an important element of
strong modeling and may appear at any
stage of the process. This creativity may
be reected in how a problem is framed,
how key factors are identi 2ed, or how
models are constructed and adapted. It
can also appear in the way teams combine
methods, incorporate real?world consid?
erations, or communicate their results.
Judges value originality when it is 
purposeful. It is not about unnecessary
complexity, but about choices that improve
the model and its applications. The most
successful teams demonstrate creativity by
approaching the problem with curiosity
and a willingness to explore ideas beyond
the most obvious solution paths.
Emergency evacuation sweep problems
like Problem A are challenging and 
involve reconciling competing objectives
under uncertainty. Unlike optimization
problems with clear single objectives,
these problems require consideration
of factors such as: minimizing total clear?
 CONSORTIUM 130 Spring/Summer                                  
HiMCM Contest
82
ance time, prioritizing vulnerable occupants,
balancing responder workloads, and 
accounting for unpredictable hazards like
spreading 2re, structural collapse, and 
occupant behavior under stress. Creative
modeling approaches move beyond deter?
ministic models to capture the tail ?risk 
(unlikely) scenarios that are important in
safety?critical applications. Creativity comes
from recognizing which real ?world 
complexities need to be explicitly modeled
(such as dynamic path real?time re?routing
or fatigue a-ecting responder speed) versus
which can be abstracted away; and from 
designing computational frameworks that re?
main tractable while preserving the stochastic,
time?dependent nature of actual emergencies.
Paper 16603 (Example 40) demonstrates 
creativity through an innovative "Optimize?
then?Simulate" hybrid framework that 
combines deterministic optimization with
stochastic simulation. Rather than treating
the evacuation sweep problem as purely 
deterministic or probabilistic, the team 
separates the problem into three computa?
tional stages: K?Means clustering for zone
assignment, TSP (Traveling Salesperson
Problem) heuristics for path planning, and
Monte Carlo simulation with path2nding
for uncertainty modeling (referred to as A*
modeling in the paper). They recognize that
while optimal routes can be pre?computed
under ideal conditions, actual emergency
sweeps require dynamic adaptation to 
blockages, smoke, and rescue needs. The
models capability to distinguish between
2re scenarios (higher baseline speed, smoke
penalties, zero blockages) and earthquake
scenarios (lower speed, structural blockages,
clear visibility) through straightforward 
parameter toggling demonstrates an elegant
design for reusability across emergency types.
For Problem B, the judges expected 
certain types of models (such as AHP , EWM,
PCA and TOPSIS) to be used and were 
impressed when teams employed unique
strategies or model types. Team 16785 
(Example 41) used a two?stage machine
learning framework to create their
model.  Team 16911 (Example 42) created
three machine learning models to 
determine the best city to host in 2029.
While Team 16521 (Example 43) used
PCA and they added a geographic factor
to their ecological footprint index to 
incorporate regional differences.  
Additionally, they inventively used a
Borda count to establish a 2nal ranking.
The judges also enjoyed seeing teams
use novel techniques in other steps of
the modeling process. For example,
Team 16947 (Example 44) explained
steps a city could take to improve their
ability to be chosen as a sustainable host
but then went further and picked a city
that had ranked in the middle and
showed how the city’s ranking would
improve if they applied some of the 
suggested strategies. Team 16739 
(Example 45) employed ARIMA to 
predict the characteristics of the cities
in 2029 to plug into their model.
Citations, References, AI
Usage Report
Teams must give credit where credit is
due.  It is okay to use existing models
found elsewhere, but teams that do this
but do not cite, lose the trust of the
judges.  Teams that use existing models
must cite their source(s) within the paper
at the point they present the model and
include a reference citation in the back
of the paper.  This is also true for all graphs
and tables taken from literature.  All data,
figures, graphs, and tables that come from
outside sources require documentation at
the point in the paper that they appear.
Lack of documentation will result in a lower
designation.  Teams should use “in line”
documentation with footnotes or endnotes
to give proper credit to outside sources.  
With the increasing availability of AI tools,
COMAP recognizes that teams may use
these resources to support their modeling
process and the preparation of their 
submissions. Any use of AI must be 
treated as an external source and clearly 
documented in an AI Use Report. This 
documentation should provide transparency
into how the tool was used by including 
the excerpts of prompts and outputs.
Teams have used AI tools in a variety of
ways, such as gathering or summarizing
data, exploring coding approaches, or 
generating potential modeling strategies.
When used thoughtfully, these tools can
support and enhance the modeling 
process. However, reliance on AI without
careful evaluation can weaken a submission.
Like any resource, AI outputs should be
approached critically. Teams are responsible
for any AI-generated content they choose
to incorporate. Thoughtful and responsible
use of AI strengthens both the 
credibility and quality of a submission.
Final Thoughts
On behalf of COMAP and the contest
judges, we would like to again thank all
advisors and students for their partici ?
pation in the HiMCM.  Each year the
quality and level of mathematics 
demonstrated by the high school student
teams amazes and impresses our judges.
We truly enjoy reading all solution
papers.  Successful teams use a wide 
variety and level of mathematics.  While
teams using post ?high school/under ?
graduate level mathematics are in a
league of their own if they understand
and can explain their work, teams using
basic high school mathematics and much
simpler approaches are oen among our
Outstanding designees.  We encourage
students of all levels to compete in 
future HiMCM competitions as well as
our ICM/MCM contests targeted to 
undergraduates.  To be successful, read
the comments and guidance provided
in this article, see the TIPS article 
on the COMAP website, and visit:
www.comap.org/mathmodels 
to review previous problems.

Problem Chosen
A
2025
HiMCM
Summary Sheet
Team Control Number
16486
Building sweep is the process that responders verifying each area in a building is clear of occupants
during emergencies [1] such as ﬁres or gas leaks. This process is complex due to the variation of
buildings’ size, layout, occupants’ distribution. Smoke, blocked corridors, and limited communication
further complicate search operations. Our goal is to build a quantitative model that assigns responder
teams to routes which clear all required rooms quickly and safely, while accounting for room priorities
and possible redundancy.
To mathematically model building sweep problem, we convert each building layout to a node-edge
graph: rooms, corridor junctions, stair landings and exits are nodes; walkable connections are edges
with traversal times. Required rooms are evaluated on sweep times and priority weights through
0–3 rating scores in four dimensions of room function, hazard proximity, occupant vulnerability, and
lingering tendency. A mixed-integer linear program (MILP) [2] is designed to solve our model, which
decides each team’s route and the time each room becomes “all clear”, to minimize a combination of
sweep ﬁnishing time, total traversal time, and a priority-weighted summation of room clearance times.
Our model framework is general that is able to take any ﬂoor plan and team number into consid-
erations and return optimal sweep routes. We apply it to three building layouts: a basic one-storey
oﬃce, a ﬁve-ﬂoor school, and a single-family house. In both the school and house, the optimizer
naturally divides the layout into intuitive “zones” (upper / middle / lower ﬂoors, or bedroom wing /
living-basement / kitchen–garage) and assigns each zone to one team, yielding simple, non-overlapping
paths that match how incident commanders already think.
Our experiments provide quantitative evidence for the eﬀectiveness of our model. On the school
layout, we compare four strategies—random assignment, greedy nearest-room, manual ﬂoor splits, and
our MILP strategy. For the same number of responders, the MILP strategy consistently achieves the
shortest sweep time, especially when only one or two responders are available. A second experiment
uses the single house to study the eﬀect of priority weights: with moderate weights, high-risk rooms
are cleared noticeably earlier with almost no change in total sweep time; with stronger “peak-hour”
weights, their average clearance time improves by about 40% at the cost of a longer overall sweep.
Our main contributions and innovations are:
1. A uniﬁed graph-based MILP sweep framework and algorithm that turn raw ﬂoor plans into
optimal multi-team routes with built-in redundancy.
2. An interpretable four-factor room-priority system and soft-deadline extension that make the
question “who should we save ﬁrst?” explicit and tunable for decision-makers.
3. Experiments on realistic layouts showing that our MILP strategy outperforms random, greedy,
and manual rules and identiﬁes a practical “sweet spot” of about three responders for medium
buildings, together with extensions for time-dependent hazards and technology support.
Keywords: building sweep; evacuation planning; mixed-integer linear programming; responder
routing; graph model; room-priority weights; time-dependent hazards; technology-supported search.
Problem Chosen
B
2024
HiMCM/MidMCM
Summary Sheet
Team Control Number
#17173
Abstract
Large-scale sporting events generate substantial economic and social value, yet also impose signiﬁcant
environmental burdens through energy use, transportation, waste production, and resource demand. A
balanced assessment model must incorporate multiple sustainability factors to evaluate these impacts rig-
orously. In this paper, our team develops a quantitative decision model to evaluate the environmental
sustainability of potential Super Bowl host cities, determine the most suitable future host location, and
extend the model to broader global mega-events such as the World Cup.
First, we identiﬁed six major dimensions and twenty-four factors of environmental impact based on
greenhouse gas emissions, clean energy and energy eﬃciency, waste and recycling, water
resource management, pollution control, and biodiversity and nature conservation.H y b r i d
weighting is then established through the integration ofAnalytic Hierarchy Process (AHP) and En-
tropy Weight Method (EWM) to balance subjective judgment with objective data variance. These
weights are applied to Grey Relational Analysis (GRA) and Fuzzy Comprehensive Evaluation
(FCE) to obtain dual sustainability rankings. A temporal fairness adjustment using Principal Com-
ponent Analysis (PCA) and time-series decomposition is introduced to remove technology-era bias
and ensure equitable cross-decade comparison. After establishing adjusted and unadjusted rankings,New
Orleans, Louisiana, is found to be the most sustainable host city for the 2029 Super Bowl.
Then, the model is expanded to multi-venue international competitions, where transportation emission
between diﬀerent venues becomes prominent. By integrating sustainability scores and inter-venue travel
distance intoTOPSIS, we identify the environmentally optimal twelve-stadium conﬁguration for the 2030
World Cup across Spain, Portugal, and Morocco. Finally, short-term operational strategies and long-term
infrastructure development pathways are proposed to support future host city bids, and key distinctions
between single-venue and multi-venue events are analyzed.
We believe that the model is robust and highly generalizable to large-scale sporting event, and oﬀers a
scalable decision mechanism to guide environmentally-aware host selection.
Keywords: AHP, EWM, GRA, FCE, Time-Series Decomposition, TOPSIS, PCA, Superbowl, World
Cup, Sustainability

1.2LiteratureReview
Xiao (2025) investigates optimization of emergency evacuation strategies and fire-risk control in
vocational colleges, emphasizing institution-specific procedures and targeted mitigation measures for
campus environments [1]. Yu (2025) applies simulation to metro-station evacuation, exploring path-design
and optimization techniques tailored to confined, high-density transit spaces [2]. Guo (2024) examines
evacuation and safety-facility optimization in highway service areas, highlighting the need to adapt
evacuationplanning to roadside facility layouts and vehicular contexts [3]. Ling et al. (2023) study library
evacuationwithattentiontopedestrianspatialrequirements,demonstratinghowroomfunctionandoccupant
flowcharacteristicsshouldinformevacuationstrategiesinacademicsettings[4].Zhangetal.(2023)address
evacuationapproachesforsuperhigh-risebuildingsduringsuddenincidents,focusingonverticalcirculation,
staging, and special procedural considerations for ultra-tall environments [5]. Xie et al. (2022) develop a
pedestrian evacuation model that incorporates panic psychology, showing how emotional and cognitive
factorsalterevacuationdynamicsandinfluencemodeldesign[6].GaoandJia(2021)proposekindergarten
activity-unit design strategies from an evacuation perspective, underlining the importance of spatial layout
choices for vulnerable populations [7]. Yu et al. (2021) analyze how metro door-control strategies affect
platform evacuation efficiency, evidencing operational controls as a key lever for improving egress
performance in rail systems [8]. Huo et al. (2020) investigate staircase evacuation in primary-school
teaching buildings with consideration of children’s movement characteristics, which necessitates tailored
stair-useprotocolsandspacingdesigns[9].Huangetal.(2019)discusshospitalpersonnelevacuationunder
fire scenarios, emphasizing complexities introduced by immobile or assisted occupants and the need for
medical-awareevacuationplans[10].
Wang(2019)appliesmaximum-flowconceptstooptimizereversiblelanesettings,demonstratingthat
traffic-levelflowmodelscansupportemergencyroutingdecisionsonroadnetworks[11].Zhaoetal.(2018)
explore strategies for constructing urban comprehensive safety systems, linking building-level evacuation
needstobroaderurbanresilienceandgovernanceconsiderations[12]. HanandLiu(2018)presentapath-
selectionmodelbasedonevacuationpathsetsandapplyitincrowd-evacuationsimulation,contributingan
algorithmic approach to routing under congestion [13]. Cai (2018) discusses urban design strategies for
enhancing spatial disaster prevention safety at the block level, offering spatiotemporal perspectives on
refuge and evacuation planning in urban design [14]. Zhang (2018) provides an overview of safety
evacuationinmetrostations,synthesizingoperational,structural,andmanagerialfactorspertinenttotransit-
stationegress[15].PengandShao(2016)studyfire-evacuationstrategiesforhigh-riseresidentialbuildings,
withattentiontooccupantbehaviorandverticalegresslimitations[16].Xietal.(2015)analyzepersonnel
evacuationstrategies for university teachingbuildings,reinforcing that instructional building functionand
scheduling influence evacuation patterns and protocols [17]. Li et al. (2015) propose an emergency-
evacuation framework for rail-transit hubs based on immune co-evolution, illustrating adaptive, co-
evolutionary algorithmic frameworks for large multimodal nodes [18]. Xiang et al. (2014) analyze
expressway emergency-evacuation strategies without demand prediction, highlighting tactics for rapid
clearanceunderuncertaintrafficdemand[19].Finally,Wang,He,andXiang(2014)studyoptimalstaircase
evacuationstrategiesinhigh-risebuildings,focusingonstairusepoliciesanddesignparameterscriticalto
verticalegressefficiency[20].
Collectively, these works span facility-specific optimization, occupant-centered behavioral modeling,
traffic-levelinterventions,andalgorithmic/simulationmethods.Theyestablishacomprehensivefoundation,
ranging from micro-scale room and stair dynamics to macro-scale urban resilience that the present study
buildsupon.Notably,whilemanystudiesaddressevacuationtiming,spatialdesign,andoccupantbehavior
across contexts, relatively few explicitly model coordinated responder-centric room-by-room sweep
strategies under operational constraints such as communication failure, reduced visibility, and prioritized
room checking; this gap motivates the responder-focused sweep model developed in this paper, the work
flowisshowninFigure.2.

1.2 DetailedProblemRestatementandInterpretation
To declare “all clear” for emergency evacuations, several safety and procedural conditions
mustbesatisfied:
• Responders must visually conﬁrm that no persons remain in each room.
• They must perform an audible check, calling out and reporting “clear” on their radios,
especially when visibility is poor.
• They must conduct a physical inspection of all subareas in a room (corners, behind furni-
ture, under desks when appropriate).
• They must evaluate residual hazards, including smoke, gas, or structural debris that could
still endanger safety.
Only after conﬁrming both human and environmental safety can the area be considered
truly “all clear.” The room is then clearly marked and documented over radio to prevent
re-entry and redundant searches.
When responders encounter remaining individuals, those individuals must be assisted or
carried to safety and transferred to medical personnel. Natural randomness in disasters is
inevitable. A path that appears optimal in advance may suddenly be blocked by debris or
smoke. Responders then attempt to navigate around blocked paths, and in worst cases, may
be delayed enough that some occupants cannot be reached in time.
We assume all ﬁrst responders entering the building are equally healthy, fast, and strong,
having been rigorously trained under standard procedure. Other personnel remain on the
outer perimeter, supporting triage and treatment once victims are brought outside.
The nature of occupants also plays a critical role in sweep dynamics. Key factors include
occupant weight, the number of occupants per room, and the fraction of children or elderly.
Facilities with children, such as daycares or schools, require responders to search every corner
carefully and assist young individuals who may be hiding. To avoid misinterpreting injuries
and needs, responders assist all remaining occupants. Each setting demands a tailored
approachthataccountsforroomlayout,hallwaydistances,occupantbehavior,andbuilding
density.
Thetypeofemergencyadditionallyinfluencessweepoperations. Fires,earthquakes,and
otherhazardscreatedistinctconditionsthataffectbothresponderandoccupantbehavior.
Infires,heat,flames,andsmokemustbeavoided,oftenforcingresponderstoprioritizeacces-
sibleroomsfirst. Earthquakescancausestructuraldamage,blockexits,andcreateunstable
environments,requiringsweepsthataccountfordebrisandalternativeescaperoutes. Each
hazardmodifiesoccupantevacuationspeed,choiceofescaperoutes,andrespondertactics.
Ourmodelthereforeexplicitlydistinguishesbetweenfireandearthquakescenarios.
Team # 16521 Page 4 of 28
2 ProblemAnalysis
Inordertounderstandhowamajorsportingeventgeneratesenvironmentalpressure,wefirstreviewed
foundationalworksontheenvironmentalassessmentofmega-events,includingCollinsetal.[6]andPour-
pakdelfekr&Oboudi[21].ThesestudiesemphasizethatlargeeventssuchastheOlympics,FIFAWorld
Cup,andnationalchampionshipsimposesubstantialecologicalburdensthroughenergyconsumption,
transportation,wateruse,andwaste. Theyalsohighlightthattheseimpactsareunevenlydistributed
acrosslocations,makingenvironmentalsustainabilityinherentlydependentonhost-citycharacteristics.
Motivatedbytheseinsights,weadoptanecologicalfootprintviewwithastrongemphasisoncarbon
dioxide–equivalentemissionsasaquantitativemetricforcomparingpotentialhostcities.
KeyEnvironmentalImpactAreas
Acrosstheliterature,severalimpactdomainsconsistentlyappearastheprimarydriversofanevent’s
environmentalfootprint.Energyconsumptionisoneofthemostcriticalcomponents,particularlywhen
combinedwiththecarbonintensityofthehostregion’spowergrid. Transportationemissions—largely
drivenbylong-distancetravelbyspectators,teams,andstaﬀ—arerepeatedlyidentiﬁedasthedominant
contributortoevent-levelgreenhouse-gasemissions[27]. Waterusageandwaterscarcityconcernsalso
playanimportantrole,especiallyinaridregionsorstadiumswithhighirrigationdemands. Solid-
wastegeneration,includingfoodwasteandpackaging,furthercontributestotheevent’senvironmental
burden,asnotedinPourpakdelfekr&Oboudi’sreview[21].Thesefourdomainscreateacomprehensive
structureforassessingenvironmentalsustainabilityconsistentlyandcomparablyacrosscities.
Location-DependentVariability
Akeythemeemergingfrompriorresearchisthatenvironmentalimpactvariessharplybylocation.
AsCollinsetal.[6]argue,thesustainabilityproﬁleof a host city is shaped by several geographic and
infrastructuralfactors.Diﬀerencesinelectricitygeneration(suchastheshareofrenewableenergy),local
climate,buildingenergydemand,andregionaltransitsystemsallinﬂuencethe environmentalburden
ofalargeevent. Forexample,citieswithcleanerpowergridsinherentlygeneratefeweroperational
emissions,whileregionswithcoldclimatesmayrequirelesscoolingenergy. Publictransitavailability
aﬀectsthe extentof car-based travel,and waste-diversionsystems determine howmucheventwasteis
recycledratherthanendingupinlandﬁll.Theselocation-speciﬁccharacteristicscannotbestandardized
oroﬀsetbyeventorganizers in the short term and mustbe incorporated intoanymodel that seeks to
evaluateenvironmentalsustainabilityacrossmultiplecities.
RelevancetoHostCityDecision
Giventhemultidimensionalnatureofenvironmentalimpactsandtheirsensitivitytolocalconditions,
selectingahostcityrequiresamodelingframeworkthatintegratesbothdirectecologicalburdensand
contextualgeographicfactors. Theecologicalfootprintapproachprovidesatransparentquantitative
structureforconvertingeventactivitiesintocarbon-relatedmeasuresofresourceuse,whilepriorre-
search highlights the necessity of evaluating travel patterns, energy sources, water availability,
andwastesystems[6,21]. Bysynthesizingtheseinsightsintoaunifiedassessmentframework,wecan
mean-ingfully differentiate cities based on their expected environmental performance and provide a
defensiblerecommendationforthemostsustainablehostinglocation.
Team # 16739  Page 19 of 25 
7 Comparative Analysis of Model Results 
By comparing the results from Sections 3–4 with those from Section 5, several structural differ-
ences emerge between single-event mega-events such as the Super Bowl and large-scale, multi-
venue or multi-sport competitions such as the Summer Olympic Games. The key differentiating 
mechanisms can be summarized as follows: 
(1) Temporal Structure: Short-Term Peaks vs. Long-Horizon Accumulation
In the Super Bowl model (Sections 3–4), the entropy-weighted results assign the highest weights to 
stadium water use, per-capita water consumption, spectator travel emissions, and transportation-
related emissions, followed by electricity consumption for stadiums and hotels. Construction-phase 
and long-term legacy indicators contribute almost nothing to the weighting structure. This pattern in-
dicates that the environmental pressure of a single-event competition is dominated by short-duration, 
peak-load consumption concentrated around game day. In contrast, in the Olympic model, indicators 
such as Olympic Village electricity intensity and construction/demolition waste obtain significantly 
higher entropy weights. This shift reflects that multi-venue events generate cumulative, time-extended 
sustainability pressure, where long-horizon resource use becomes more influential than short-term 
op-erational spikes. 
(2) Spatial Structure: Single-Venue Events vs. Multi-Venue Networks
In the Super Bowl model, spatially relevant factors are primarily: Average spectator travel dis-
tance, Public transport coverage, Share of green transportation. Regression results show that these 
variables strongly explain spectator and transportation-related carbon emissions. In the Olympic model, 
newly added variables—average inter-venue travel distance, in-Games venue reuse rate, and post-
Games reutilization rate—form a distinct principal component (PC3), interpreted as the “venue spatial 
configuration and legacy utilization” dimension. This component contributes meaningfully to multiple 
environmental indicators. This comparison highlights that for comprehensive multi-sport events, the 
spatial network of venues, together with the multi-tier mobility system linking venues and the Olympic 
Village, becomes a structural driver of environmental outcomes. 
(3) Shared Core Drivers: Electricity Systems and Transport Structure
Across both models, a consistent result emerges: Electricity carbon intensity, Renewable-en-
ergy share, Public transport coverage, and Green mobility proportion.All exhibit high importance 
in both the principal component regressions and the entropy weighting scheme. These variables func-
tion as common core determinants of environmental performance. However, the mechanisms differ. 
For the Super Bowl, these city attributes primarily amplify or mitigate single-day carbon footprints via 
short-term electricity demand and transportation organization. For the Olympic Games, they determine 
not only operational emissions during the 17-day event window but also the long-term emission base-
line for decades of venue and Olympic Village operation.

2 Assumptions and Justiﬁcation
Table 1: Model Assumptions
# Assumption Justiﬁcation
1 Responders have the building
layout plan in advance and
clearly know the structure such
as the location of the rooms and
the length of the corridors.
In almost all jurisdictions, ﬁre codes and public safety regulations
require building owners to submit detailed architectural plans to local
ﬁre departments and emergency planning agencies. As a result, it has
become standard practice for rescue personnel to immediately access
accurate ﬂoor plans, stairwell locations, door widths, and other critical
information before entering a building.
2 Responders maintain a constant
speed of movement as long as
roads are clear; we do not factor
in fatigue.
Typical structural sweeping during ﬁres is completed within 5 to 15
minutes. Physiological studies indicate that when using modern
breathing apparatus and protective gear, a noticeable decline in speed
due to fatigue only occurs after 15 to 20 minutes of high-intensity work.
3 Responders strictly execute their
assigned routes and do not
deviate to conduct unauthorized
rescue operations.
In professional ﬁreﬁghting doctrine, primary search is a coordinated
team operation under the uniﬁed command of the incident commander.
If individual responders are permitted to break formation, it can result in
some areas being searched repeatedly while others are completely
overlooked, signiﬁcantly increasing the likelihood of missing occupants.
4 Occupants remain stationary,
awaiting rescue in their
respective rooms.
Fire safety studies conﬁrm that untrained individuals generally remain
stationary during poor visibility. Simulating their movement would add
unnecessary complexity without improving search operation accuracy,
making the stationary model both practical and realistic.
5 Communication between
responders is instantaneous in the
base model.
The base model is intended to solve the pure routing and allocation
problem. Perfect radio communication represents the ideal condition
that incident commanders strive to maintain in emergency response
operations. Communication delays, channel overload, and radio dead
zones are real phenomena, but they are treated separately in Section 6
(Model Extensions).
6 Fire spread and smoke
obstruction are not considered in
the base model.
The primary objective of the base model is to determine optimal static
sweep routes. Introducing time-varying edge weights from ﬁre spread
would convert the problem into a complex dynamic network ﬂow. By
ﬁxing hazard conditions, we obtain a clean lower bound on sweep time;
dynamic hazards are explicitly incorporated and analyzed in Section 6.
7 The responders’ path between
rooms does not account for
corridor width.
In most buildings, corridor widths exceed 1.1 meters—the minimum
requirement for evacuation routes under the International Fire Code. At
standard search speeds, the time lost to single-ﬁle movement or brief
queuing at doorways is negligible compared to room search time and the
total travel distance along main corridors.
