(Section 2).
1.3.1 Overarching Assumptions
• Assumption 1 (Pre-informed Building Layout):We assume that the ﬁreﬁghting squad
has complete information on the layout of the building. Justiﬁcation: Evidence shows
that the sweep team is given the ﬂoor plan of the building that they are going to search
before their operation [2]. This way, our model can ﬁnd an optimal path directly based oﬀ
of a concrete ﬂoor plan, instead of opting for a dynamic model, where information about
the building is slowly uncovered. This assumption makes sense because assuming that
ﬁreﬁghters have the building plan before the actual operation will greatly increase their
chances of a successful sweep, which then leads to an increase in their chance of saving
more lives (referenced in2.1).
• Assumption 2 (Fireﬁghter Movement Speed):We assume that ﬁreﬁghters move at1.4
m/s during a sweep operation when they are at maximum stamina while wearing full
ﬁreﬁghting gear [3].
• Assumption 3 (Fireﬁghter Traversal and Search Rules):We assume that ﬁreﬁghters in
a sweep operation always take the shortest path available from room to room without any
detours. We also assume that all rooms have to be visited at least once by the ﬁreﬁghters.
Justiﬁcation: First, if a ﬁreﬁghter made up their mind to travel from one room to another,
they are going to do it directly without a diversion in their path. Second, every room
must be visited at least once because it fulﬁlls the SAR requirement for a standard sweep
operation. Thus, our interpretation of the model necessitates the thorough search of every
room in the building, and we assume that ﬁreﬁghters travel straight from one room to
another (referenced in 2.1).
• Assumption 4 (Occupant Movement Rules): We assume that, when evacuating an
occupant from a room, the ﬁreﬁghter will instruct them the safest way to the nearest exit
based on the results from the sweep. The occupant will then walk that path on their
own without a ﬁreﬁghter’s escort. Justiﬁcation: We made this assumption because it
simpliﬁes ﬁreﬁghter movement rules, as we only have to consider the optimal path for the
ﬁreﬁghter to visit each room in their part of the sweep; considering occupant escorting
will over-complicate our model (referenced in2.1).
• Assumption 5 (Crowded Hallway): We assume that we do not have to consider the
problem of a narrow hallway, and the possibility of ﬁreﬁghters crowding up in the same
part of the hallway at the same time of their sweep as they traverse from one room to
another. Justiﬁcation: We disregard this possibility because it will over-complicate our
routing optimization. If two or more ﬁreﬁghters do end up in the same part of a hallway
at the same time, they can sort the problem of how to walk past each other out on the spot
without taking long. So, this problem overcomplicates our model, and it also has little
impact on the ﬁnal result (referenced in2.1).
• Assumption 6 (Deﬁning Sweep Time): We deﬁne the total time of a sweep operation
as the time taken by the ﬁreﬁghter who has the slowest route. Justiﬁcation: This is
because the sweep operation ends when all ﬁreﬁghters leave the building through an exit.
Therefore, the time taken by the last ﬁreﬁghter to complete their sweep route is the total
time for the sweep (referenced in2).
 6 / 25 
Team # 17205 
2.3 Key Behavioral and Data Assumptions 
(1) Steady attendance and local proportion.
The total in-person attendance, combined with the proportion of local spectators vs. non-local spectators
are treated as the same across cities. Justification. Ca pacity and demand for the Super Bowl are similar for 
qualified venues; fixing these isolates the effect of location and infrastructure.  
(2) A stable origin distribution.
We assume a regional distribution of non-local spectators similar to recent Super Bowls, and independent
of host city. Justification. Attendance is dominated by a relatively stable national fan and sponsor base. It will 
be fair to compare using a common distribution. 
(3) Simplified long distance mode of choice.
Shares of travel mode are defined by distance bands (short: mostly car/rail; medium: mixed; long: mainly
air) with fixed emission factors for each mode. Justification. Captures the primary drivers of travel emissions 
without individual-level data requirement.  
(4) Local mobility based on local mobility in a city.
We assume that the travel distance per spectator by lo cality per crowd has si milar proportions between
the cities, and the proportion of public tran sit vs private vehicles for city residents with respect to it is city-
specific. Justification. Urban scale is comparably sized across candidate metros; variations are largely driven 
by transit systems, which our model is based on.  
(5) Standardization of stay, Hotels and per capita consumption.
Non-local spectators are considered to stay a co mmon number of nights, and per room-night emissions
along with per capita waste/water production are assumed to have corresponding, the treatment results being 
contingent on the grid mix, waste management, and water use of the individual cities. Justification. Detailed 
micro-data are not available; unified baselines including intensity factors linked to the individual city are used 
to maintain comparability.

4 Graph-basedSweepModel
4.1 Frombuildinglayoutstonodegraph
Tomathematicallymodeltheproblem,werepresentthelayoutofabuildingasagraph𝐺=(𝑉,𝐸).
Nodes 𝑉arediscretelocationssuchasroominteriors,hallway,stairlandings,andentrancesorexits.
Edges(𝑖,𝑗)∈𝐸 arefeasiblemovementsbetweennodes,forexamplehallways,andstairﬂights.Let
𝑅⊆𝑉bethesetofroomsthatmustbeswept.Foreachroom𝑖∈𝑅wehave
• a sweep time𝑠𝑖 > 0, the time for a team to fully search room𝑖;
Team 16486 Page 8
• a risk or priority weight 𝑤𝑖 > 0, indicating how costly it is to leave room𝑖 uncleared for a long
time.
For each edge(𝑖, 𝑗)∈ 𝐸 we deﬁne a traverse time 𝑡𝑖𝑗 > 0, derived from the geometric distance, stair
diﬃculty and local conditions (smoke, obstacles). In this way𝑡𝑖𝑗 is an edge-based quantity, while𝑤𝑖 is
a node-based quantity.
We consider a set of responder teams𝐾 = {1,..., |𝐾|}. Team 𝑘 ∈ 𝐾 starts from an exit node.
From this graph-based description, the sweep-planning problem becomes a combinatorial routing
and assignment task on 𝐺: we must decide which edges each team traverses, which rooms each
team is responsible for, and in what order rooms are cleared, while respecting timing and redundancy
requirements. These decisions are naturally modeled by binary variables (on edge usage and room
assignments) coupled with continuous variables (for arrival and clearance times). This structure makes
a mixed-integer linear programming (MILP) [7]formulation a natural choice: it can capture network
ﬂow, timing consistency, and room-coverage constraints in a linear way, while allowing us to compute
globally optimal sweep plans for the layouts we study.
Basic oﬃce scenario. Figure 3 illustrates the conversion for the basic six–oﬃce hallway. The left
panel shows the ﬂoor plan with six identical rooms𝑅1–𝑅6, a central hallway𝐻, and two exits𝐸1 and
𝐸2. The right panel shows the resulting node graph.

Figure 3: Conversion from ﬂoor plan to node graph for the basic oﬃce scenario. Left: physical layout

The conversion follows three simple rules:
• Each room interior (here𝑅1–𝑅6) becomes a room node in𝑉.
• Distinct positions along circulation spaces (the hallway) and at exits become hallway nodes;i n
this layout we use one hallway node in front of each pair of doors and one node at each exit.
• We add an undirected edge (represented as two directed edges in 𝐸) between any two nodes
that a responder can walk between without passing through another door or junction: along the
hallway between neighbouring hallway nodes, and between each room node and its adjacent
hallway node.
All traverse times𝑡𝑖𝑗 in this basic scenario are then derived from geometric distances between these
nodes (e.g. 5 m between adjacent hallway nodes at 1 m/s gives𝑡𝑖𝑗 = 5 s).

Flow conservation. To ensure that each team’s 𝑥𝑘
𝑖𝑗 variables describe a single continuous route
starting from 𝑠𝑘, we impose standard ﬂow-conservation constraints. Intuitively, these constraints say
that, for every intermediate node and every team, the number of edges used to enter the node equals
the number of edges used to leave it, so a team cannot “teleport” or break its path. At the start node
𝑠𝑘 we allow one extra departure so that the route can begin there. The resulting constraints follow the
usual vehicle-routing formulations.
4.4 Objective function
The sweep plan should ﬁnish early (for the goal of clearance in the shortest possible time) [8], avoid
unnecessary walking(for the goal of responder eﬃciency), and clear high-risk rooms quickly(for the
goal of prioritizing occupant safety). We capture these priorities with the linear objective [9].

The ﬁrst term is the overall completion time; minimising it pushes the operation to end as early as
possible. The second term is total traverse time over all teams and all edges used, discouraging long or
ineﬃcient routes. The third term is a risk-weighted sum of room clearance times: a room with larger
𝑤𝑖 contributes more if it is cleared late, so the optimiser is encouraged to visit high-risk rooms earlier.

4.6 Application to the basic oﬃce layout
For the one-storey oﬃce building in Figure 3 we have six identical oﬃces and two exits connected
by a straight hallway. All oﬃces have the same sweep time𝑠𝑖 = 𝑠oﬃce and a common moderate weight
𝑤𝑖 = 𝑤oﬃce. Hallway segments between adjacent doors are usually 5 m apart, so with a nominal
walking speed of 1 m/s each has 𝑡𝑖𝑗 = 5 s; door edges are assigned a short traversal time of 1 s. We
assume each oﬃce sweep requires 15 s
Because all rooms are identical in both geometry and risk, the objective (7) essentially reduces to
minimising 𝑇ﬁnish and total walking time. Solving the MILP with two responder teams starting from
the two exits yields a symmetric plan: each team sweeps the three rooms closest to its entrance, and
the optimal ﬁnishing time is𝑇ﬁnish = 60 s under the assumed parameters. We use this scenario mainly
to validate the model before applying it to more complex layouts.

6.1 Comparison of sweep strategies on the school layout
To demonstrate the beneﬁt of optimization over certain sweep rules, we evaluate four strategies on
the school layout shown in Figure 5. The building has four ﬂoors, each with a central hallway and
ﬁve classrooms, connected vertically by a single stairwell. All responders start from the ground-ﬂoor
entrance (node S1). Walking time is proportional to corridor distance and each classroom requires a
ﬁxed service time of one unit once reached.
We compare four strategies:
• RND (random): randomly permute rooms and split them evenly across responders.
• GRD (greedy): at each step send a free responder to the nearest uncleared room.
• SPLIT (ﬂoor split): manually assign contiguous ﬂoors to responders (e.g. for 𝐾 = 2, lower vs.
upper ﬂoors).
• MILP (proposed): the exact model from Section 4, minimising weighted clearance time/summationtext.1
𝑖 𝑤𝑖𝑇𝑖.

are averaged over 2,000 simulation runs; SPLIT and MILP are deterministic.

3.4 ClusteringAlgorithm
Ourgreedyworkload-balancedclusteringalgorithmassignsroomstorespondersbasedoncur-
rentworkloadandproximity:
1. Initialize responder positions at distinct exits
2. Set workload Wi = 0 for all respondersi
3. While unassigned rooms remain:
(a) Select responder i∗ =a r gm i ni Wi with minimum workload
(b) Among unassigned rooms, selectr∗ =a r gm i nr d(r,posi) nearest to responderi∗
(c) Assign room r∗ to responder i∗
(d) Update workload: Wi∗ = Wi∗ + sr∗ + d(r∗,posi∗)
(e) Update position: posi∗ = r∗
Thedistanced(r1,r2)istheshortestpathtraveltimebetweennodes,computedusingDijkstra’s
algorithmonthebuildinggraph[3].
Thisgreedyapproachhastwokeyproperties: (1)itmaintainsspatiallocalitybyalwaysas-
signing the nearest room, reducing travel overhead, and (2) it balances workload dynamically,
preventingsituationswhereoneresponderisoverwhelmedwhileothersﬁnish.
3.5 PathOptimizationAlgorithm
Givenaresponder’sassignedrooms, weﬁndan e ﬃcienttraversal pat husi ngamulti-stage
travelingsalesmanproblem-inspiredsolver[2,5,7,10]:
Stage1: Nearest-NeighborInitialization.Startingfromtheresponder’sinitialexit,build
apathbyrepeatedlyvisitingthenearestunvisitedroom.Thisprovidesareasonableinitialsolution
quickly[10].
Stage 2: 2-Opt Local Search. Iterativelytestswappingpairsofedgesinthepath. If
swapping edges (ri,ri+1) and (rj,rj+1) with (ri,rj) and (ri+1,rj+1) reduces total path length,
accepttheswap.Repeatuntilnoimprovingswapsexist[7].
Stage3:Or-OptImprovement.Testrelocatingsequencesofonetothreeconsecutiverooms
tootherpositionsinthepath.Thisescapeslocalminimathat2-optcannotimprove[8].
Stage4: 2-OptRe inement.Apply2-optagaintorefineimprovementsfromOr-opt[7].
Thiscombinationofnearest-neighborinitializationandlocalsearchimprovementstypically
producespathswithin5percentofoptimalwhilerunninginpolynomialtime[2,4,5,7,10].

3 Model I: Baseline Sweep Time Model
To address the complex dynamics of an evacuation sweep, our model is separated into three
computational stages: K-Means clustering, TSP routing, and Monte Carlo simulation with
A* pathﬁnding. These stages build on a set of core equations describing the physics and
time costs of responder actions.
3.1 Stage 0: Core Equations (Physics Engine)
3.1.1 Total Sweep Time
For a single responder k, the total sweep time is modeled as
Tk = Ttravel,k +Tsearch,k +Tassist,k +Tdoor,k +Tmark,k +Tcomm,k, (1)
where each term aggregates the contributions from all relevant events (movement, searches,
rescues, doors, marking, and communication).

vcarry
+ dreturn
veﬀ(t), (11)
added to Tk whenever a rescue occurs.
3.1.8 Communication and Marking Times
Communication time is Tcomm ∼ Uniform(1,3) s, applied when a blockage is reported, and
is sampled from the uniform distribution between its observed min and max.
3.2 Stage 1: K-Means Clustering (Assigning Zones)
To assign zones, we represent the building as a graph G =( N,E ) where nodes N are room
centers and key intersections, and edgesE represent traversable connections with associated
distances. Node coordinates (xi,yi) are used to cluster rooms into K zones. K-mean solves:
min
μ1,...,μK
K∑
k=1
∑
xi∈Zk
‖xi −μk‖2 (12)
to produce compact, geographically coherent zones. This yields balanced workloads and
reduced travel distances.
3.3 Stage 2: Ideal Path Optimization (TSP)
Within each cluster Zk, we solve a TSP variant: ﬁnd a path that starts at the responder’s
exit, visits all rooms in Zk exactly once, and minimizes travel distance. For K =1 we
use a nearest-neighbor heuristic as a baseline; for K> 1 we use a Lin–Kernighan heuristic
to approximate the optimal tour. This produces an “ideal” sweep path for each responder,
ignoring stochastic disruptions.
Team # 16603 Page 14 of 25
3.4 Stage 3: Monte Carlo Simulation
To model real-world uncertainty, we simulate K responders following their TSP paths in
parallel over NMC runs. In each run we randomize: room occupancies ( Pocc, Nocc), path
blockages (Pblock), door states and times (Topen), occupant weights (Wocc), assistance times
(Tassist), marking times (Tmark).
For each run, we computeTk and Tclear =m a xk Tk. Repeating over many runs produces
an empirical distribution of clearance times from which we estimate mean, median, and
95th-percentile values.
3.5 A* Pathﬁnding in the Simulation
Responders do not blindly follow precomputed TSP routes. When unexpected conditions
arise, they use A* pathﬁnding.
3.5.1 Trigger 1: Path Blockage
When traversing an edge (u,v), a Bernoulli trial with probability Pblock determines if the
path is blocked (earthquake scenarios). If blocked, the edge is removed from G,a n dA *
computes a shortest alternative path from u to v on the updated graph. The travel time
along this new path is added to Tk.
3.5.2 Trigger 2: Evacuee Transport
When an occupant is found, the responder pauses the TSP route and uses A* to ﬁnd the
shortest path to their assigned exit. The cost function uses vcarry while carrying and veﬀ for
the return. The computed travel time plus Tassist are added to Tk.
3.6 Optimize Number of Responders ( K)
Increasing K decreases the load per responder but eventually yields diminishing returns. To
ﬁnd the optimal K,w e :
1. Run low-ﬁdelity Monte Carlo simulations (1,000 runs) for K =1 ,2,...,K max.
2. For each K, compute T95(K), the 95th-percentile clearance time.
3. Plot T95(K) vs. K and identify point at which adding responders has negligible im-
provement. Thusm the K at the elbow of this plot is selected as the optimal staﬃng
level.
3.7 Final High-Fidelity Simulation
After choosing Kopt, we run a high-ﬁdelity Monte Carlo simulation with NMC = 100,000
runs. This provides a robust estimate of the full clearance-time distribution for the optimal
staﬃng level, including rare but critical tail events.

5.3 Step2:TheCombinedWeightingModel
ThedeterminationofindicatorweightsisacriticalstepinanyMCDMmodel, asitdefinestherelative
importanceofeachcriterioninthefinalevaluation. Tocreateabalancedandrobustweightingschemethat
mitigatesthebiasesofanysinglemethod,wecombineasubjectiveapproach(AnalyticNetworkProcess)
withanobjectiveapproach(EntropyWeightMethod).
5.3.1 SubjectiveWeighting:AnalyticNetworkProcess(ANP)
Tocaptureexpertopinionsonstrategicprioritiesandthecomplexinterdependenciesbetweencriteria,we
employ the ANP. Unlike the simpler AHP, ANP allows us to model a network of influences, which is
suitableforourfour-stageframeworkwhereeachstagecaninfluencetheothers.
Team # 16738 Page 12 of 25
Step 1: Construct the Network Structure and Judgment Matrices. We ﬁrst established the network
structure, deﬁning the relationships between our four primary indicators (Supply, Consumption, Load,
Capacity). Subsequently, simulating an expert panel, we performed pairwise comparisons using Saaty’s 1-9
scale to quantify the relative importance between connected criteria. This process generated the necessary
judgment matrices. The following table shows how we make the comparisons:
aij aji Interpretation
1 1 Criterion i andj are equally important
3 1
3 Criterion i is slightly more important thanj
5 1
5 Criterion i is moderately more important thanj
7 1
7 Criterion i is strongly more important thanj
9 1
9 Criterion i is extremely more important thanj
The following graph and tables show the comparisons of indicators
Figure 5.2: Relative importance heatmap
Step 2: Compute the Supermatrix and Final ANP Weights. The pairwise comparisons are used to
form an unweighted supermatrix, which represents the initial inﬂuence levels. This is then stochasticized
to create the weighted supermatrix, ¯W. To capture the total long-term inﬂuences, we calculate the limit
supermatrix by raising the weighted supermatrix to a large power until it converges:
¯W∞ = lim
k→∞
¯Wk (5.12)
The ﬁnal ANP weight vector,WANP , is extracted from the converged limit supermatrix.
A matrixA =( aij) must satisfy the consistency condition, checked by the Consistency Ratio (CR):
CR = CI
RI = (λmax −n)/(n−1)
RI (5.13)
where λmax is the principal eigenvalue of the matrix andRI is the Random Consistency Index.
After processing the judgment matrices and calculating the limit supermatrix, the resulting ANP weight
vector was found in Table 5.1. The overall consistency ratio for the model was calculated as CR < 0.1,
indicating that our judgments are acceptably consistent.

5.3.2 Objective Weighting: Entropy Weight Method
To incorporate the information inherent in the data itself, we use the Entropy Weight Method. This method
assigns higher weights to indicators that exhibit greater variation across the candidate cities, as they provide
more information for diﬀerentiation. The process uses the Decision MatrixX =( xij) from Step 1 as input.
Step 1: Normalize and Standardize the Data. First, each elementxij in the decision matrix is normalized
to calculate its proportionpij within its indicator column:
pij = xij∑ m
i=1 xij
(5.14)
where m is the number of cities.
Step 2: Calculate Information Entropy. The information entropyEj for each indicatorj is calculated,
which measures the degree of uncertainty or variation in the data:
Ej = −k
m∑
i=1
pij ln(pij), wherek = 1
ln(m) (5.15)
Step 3: Calculate Objective Weights. The ﬁnal entropy weightwj for each indicator is derived from its
information entropy. A lower entropy (less uncertainty, more variation) results in a higher weight.
wj = 1−Ej∑ n
j=1(1−Ej) (5.16)
This process yields the objective weight vector,WEntropy .
WEntropy =[ 0.18,0.08,0.06,0.11,0.13,0.22,0.09,0.03,0.05,0.02,0.01,0.02]
Notably, ’Travel Footprint’ received the highest objective weight, indicating that the candidate cities exhibit
the greatest variation on this metric.
5.3.3 Sigmoid Function for Dealing with Extreme Data Value
If some values reach an extreme value, the weights we have calculated before will not describe the whole
story. We designated a threshold value that indicate the shift between constant weights and dynamic weights.
This smooth dynamic adjustment mechanism is processed using a Sigmoid function. The following pairwise
function shows how it works:
λ(x)=
⎧
⎪⎪⎨
⎪⎪⎩
λbase,x < T start
λbase +(λmax −λbase)· 1
1+ e−k(x−Ttrigger),T start ≤ x ≤ Tend
λmax,x > T end
(5.17)
In whichTtrigger is the threshold value;λmax −λbase shows the range of ﬂuctuation of the weights.
Team # 16738 Page 14 of 25
5.3.4 Weight Combination
We synthesize the subjective weights from ANP (WANP ) and the objective weights from the Entropy method
(WEntropy ) using a linear combination to produce our ﬁnal, balanced weight vector,Wfinal :
Wfinal,j = λ·WANP,j +(1 −λ)·WEntropy,j (5.18)
Wesetthecombinationcoefficientλ=0.5,grantingequalimportancetoboththeexpert-drivenstrategic
priorities and the inherent data structure. This balanced approach ensures our final weights are both
strategicallyrelevantanddata-informed.
Team # 16738 Page 17 of 25
6.2 DynamicDataCorrection:Projecting2029IndicatorsviaTrendExtrapolation
Assessingthesustainabilityperformanceofcitiesin2029requirestakingintoaccounttheeffectsofpolicy
directionsonurbandevelopment.
Weidentifiedtwosecondaryindicatorsfromourframeworkthataremostlikelytochangesignificantly
overafive-yearhorizonduetoestablished,long-termpublicpolicy:
• Grid Cleanliness: Most states and major utility companies have legally mandated Renewable Portfolio
Standards (RPS) with speciﬁc targets for future years (e.g., "50% renewable by 2030").
• Municipal Waste Management Capability: Many major cities have adopted "Zero Waste" plans
with explicit goals for increasing waste diversion rates over time.
For these two indicators, we projected their 2029 values using a policy-based linear extrapolation method.
We researched the oﬃcial sustainability targets for each candidate city (or its state/region). The projected
indicator value for 2029 was then calculated as a linear interpolation between its current value and its future
policy target.
I2029 = Icurrent +(Itarget −Icurrent)× 2029− Yearcurrent
Yeartarget − Yearcurrent
(6.1)
For cities without explicit targets, we used a conservative linear extrapolation based on their historical data
from the past decade. This process resulted in two distinct decision matrices: a2024 Baseline Matrixusing
the most recent available data, and a 2029 Projected Matrix with updated values for these two dynamic
indicators.
We ran our full MCDM model (ANP-Entropy weighting and TOPSIS evaluation) twice: once on the baseline
matrix to establish a current ranking, and once on the projected matrix to forecast the 2029 sustainability
landscape.
The results, presented in Table 6.2, reveal a dynamic shift in the rankings. The table shows each city’s rank
based on 2024 data, its projected rank for 2029, the resulting change in rank, and the primary factor driving
that change.
6.3 Results Analysis and Final Recommendation
Analysis of Rankings
Proactive environmental policy strongly inﬂuences rankings. Cities in states with aggressive decarboniza-
tion goals, such as Los Angeles and the New York area, maintain or improve their positions. Notable
projected improvements occur in Baltimore and New Orleans, which are actively implementing new poli-
cies. Conversely, cities with slower energy transitions, like Dallas, see relative declines, while cities facing
structural challenges, such as Las Vegas and Phoenix with extreme water stress, remain low, highlighting
inherent geographical disadvantages.

Archetypes of Sustainability: Top Performers
The 2029 projected rankings reveal multiple paths to top sustainability scores. Our model identiﬁes three

This archetypal analysis provides a crucial strategic insight for the NFL: the deﬁnition of a "sustainable
host" is not monolithic. The choice is between a city that is inherently sustainable (Seattle), one that is
engineered to be sustainable (New York), or one that is becoming sustainable (Baltimore). This nuanced
understanding allows for a more strategic selection process aligned with the NFL’s long-term sustainability
goals.

Final Recommendation for 2029
To provide our ﬁnal recommendation, we apply a real-world constraint: the NFL’s informal "cooling-oﬀ
period," deﬁned as a 5-year window. Cities that hosted or are scheduled to host between 2025 and 2028
(New Orleans, Santa Clara/SF Bay Area, Las Vegas) are thus ineligible for the 2029 event.
Eligible Cities: Previous Hosts and New Cities. Among previous hosts, the top-ranked eligible city
is the Los Angeles Area (Inglewood), whose aggressive clean energy policies and strong infrastructure
make it the most sustainable choice within that group. Among new candidate cities,Seattle emerges as the
top performer overall, driven by the nation’s cleanest electricity grids and robust environmental policies,
followed byDenver and Baltimore, both showing strong potential and outperforming many previous hosts.
Overall Recommendation: Based on our comprehensive environmental sustainability model, the most
sustainable choice for the 2029 Super Bowl is Seattle, WA. However, if the NFL prioritizes a city with
recent hosting experience, the Los Angeles Area is the best option from that pool. We recommend
considering these two cities as the primary candidates, as both signal a strong commitment to environmental
responsibility.

4.3 Results and Analysis 
In this subsection, we use simulation results to quantitatively elucidate the relationship between 
environmental impacts and city features. 
The correlation analysis between city features and environmental impacts is shown in Fig. 1. It 
reveals that host-city features, such as climate, transport systems, and energy mix exhibit strong cor-
relations with event-period outcomes including energy use, water consumption, and carbon emissions. 
In particular, attendees’ average travel distance is strongly positively correlated with audience- and 
transport-related  emissions, whereas public-transit coverage and the share of green/low-carbon 
travel exert a suppressing effect on transport emissions. 
Figure 1  Heatmap of correlations between city features and environmental impacts 
 These patterns indicate that a host city’s transportation conditions and spatial configuration are 
key determinants of the environmental footprint of mega-sport events like the Super Bowl. 
Figure 2 Principal components’ explained-
variance plot 
Figure 3 PCA loading heatmap 
As shown in Figure 2, the first three principal components jointly explain over 85% of the total 
variance in the city-feature variables. Therefore, we retain the top three PCs as composite city-feature 

Team # 16739  Page 9 of 25 
indices for subsequent modeling. Figure 3 displays the loadings of each feature on the PCs: PC1 is 
chiefly driven by population density, public-transit coverage, and the share of green/low-carbon travel, 
and can be interpreted as a “transport & urban intensity” factor. PC2 reflects climate conditions and 
grid carbon intensity, i.e., a “climate–energy structure” factor. PC3 has the highest loading on popula-
tion density, confirming this as its core feature. 
Figure 4 Decomposition of principal-com-
ponent regression coefficients 
Figure 5 Standardized residual distributions 
for each environmental indicator 
Figure 4 further shows that among the three latent dimensions extracted by PCA, the infrastruc-
ture & energy-structure component (PC1) primarily governs resource-use and waste-related indicators; 
the climate–distance component (PC2) dominates energy use and audience-related ଶ emissions; 
and the mobility /density component (PC3) plays an important supplementary role in transport- and 
audience-related ଶ. The residual distributions in Figure 5 are centered near zero with limited dis-
persion, indicating no systematic bias in the principal-component–based model and supporting its abil-
ity to reasonably characterize differences in environmental performance across host cities. 
Figure 6 Importance ranking of environmental-impact drivers 
When considering all environmental impact indicators ଽ, grid carbon intensity, the share of 
renewable energy, and the greenness of the transport system are the location traits with the most sys-
tem-wide influence; next in importance are temperature and the level of public transit, while precipi-
tation and other climatic details have comparatively weak average effects. Specifically: 
Grid carbon intensity ଼ and renewables share  ଽ rank highest in importance, indicating that 
with the same electricity use, materials, and event operations, outcomes differ systematically under 

Team # 16739  Page 10 of 25 
high-carbon vs. low-carbon grids. Share of green travel ଺ and public-transit coverage ହ  also rank 
near the top, showing that a city’s transport mode mix jointly affects multiple environmental indicators 
(not only carbon emissions but also waste, recycling rates, water use, etc.). 
Within climate variables, temperature ଵ matters more than precipitation  ଶ : temperature is 
more tightly coupled to energy consumption and water use (HVAC loads, comfort needs), so its overall 
effect on the indicators is clear, whereas precipitation is not a core driver of cross-city differences. 
Population density ଷ and average spectator travel distance ସ have mid-to-low average ef-
fects, suggesting they are not the primary sources of variation in the environmental indicators. 
This study employs principal component analysis (PCA)  and principal component 
regression (PCR) to systematically quantify the causal relationships between city features and the 
environmen-tal impacts of hosting mega-sport events. In the subsequent model-building stage, we 
will retain the indicator system outlined above as the baseline reference and use a quantitative index 
framework to characterize the events’ environmental impacts. The indicators in this section also 
serve as references for later analyses of how differences in site features lead to variations in 
environmental impact.

4 RACE-S:Real-timePlannerforSweepingPathandResponderSetup
GivenG2,whichencodesthebuildinglayoutandpriorpropertiesofnodesandedges,weconstructaplanner,
namely Responder Action and Clearance Evacuation Solver (RACE-S), that serves two purposes: (a)
Duringan evacuation, whenever new information is revealed fromG1, it searches for optimal paths for
multiple responders using observed data and prior knowledge to (approximately) minimize sweep time
(Section 4.1,Fig.4.1). (b)Atemergencyonsetorinadvance,itoptimizesresponderlocationstobalance
shortestsweeptimeandefficientevacuationofhigh-riskroomsunderuncertaintyofG1 thatleadstodifferent
realizedsweeppaths(Section4.2,Fig.4.2).
4.1 OptimizationforSweepingPaths
4.1.1 Objective

uponinspection).Wedefine:
• Rtask ⊆ R: rooms still requiring sweep,
• sk ∈ V : current location of responder k =1 ,...,m , with the responder’s next available timetk ≥ τ.

• Tk(P): estimated completion time of responderk,
• Ci(P): estimated clearance time of roomi.
Following the contest statement, we minimize overall sweep time (makespan) while favoring early clearance

4.1.2 Solver Based on Greedy Initialization and Local Neighborhood Search
We propose an eﬀective sweep path solver, DynamicPlanner, shown in Algorithm 1 and detailed below.
Step 1: Initialize Responder Conﬁguration
A responder conﬁguration is deﬁned by
(m,Sm),S m = {s1,...,s m}⊆ R, |Sm| = m, (4.3)
where m is the number of responders and Sm is the set of distinct room nodes as starting positions. Here we
assume responders start in room nodes and(m,Sm) is given. If entering from exits, they immediately proceed
to their assigned start node.
Step 2: Greedy-algorithm-based Initial Plan
Initialization matters for an optimization algorithm. To construct an initial sweeping plan given Rtask and
the current responder states, we propose a simple greedy algorithm, denoted as PolicyGreedyInitialization:

• Select the responder who becomes available ﬁrst.

The shortest travel timeTtravel between two nodes is dynamically computed using a time-dependent variation of
Dijkstra’s algorithm [5], denoted as the function ShortestTravelTime(G2,s,i,t ) but omitted for conciseness.
Expectations are computed via Monte Carlo sampling from prior distributions. Updated Rtask or responder
states at diﬀerent time naturally alter the plan, which makes the planner real-time.
Step 3: Local-search Optimization
The greedy plan is feasible but not optimal. We reﬁne it via a local search strategy evaluating J(P)
(Eq. (4.2), denoted as a function EvaluateRoutes(P)) repeatedly with two standard routing modiﬁcations,
which widely exist in vehicle-routing literature [11]:
• Intra-route 2-opt: choose 1 ≤ a<b ≤ 𝓁k in routePk and reverseik,a ...i k,b.
• Inter-route relocate. remove a room from one route and insert it at all possible positions in another
responder’s route, retaining the best insertion.
Modiﬁcations that strictly reduceJ(P) are accepted. Iteration continues until no improvement is found.
Step 4: Updating Sweep Paths with New Information
The overall sweep path planner SweepSolver (Algorithm 3) combines greedy initialization Policy-
GreedyInitialization and local search LocalSearchOptimize. It is called at emergency onset and whenever
new information inside a room arrives, which updateRtask and responder states that can change sweep plan.

2: i ← 0
3: repeat
4: i ← i+1

4.2 Optimization for Responder Setting
When an emergency begins or even beforehand, we must determine the optimal number of responders and
their starting locations to enable the fastest sweep. This requires accounting for stochastic nature in the true
building state G1 (particularly initial occupants), and achieving strong average performance across random
realizations ofG1 sampled fromG2, denoted asGω. Thus, we formulate an upper-level optimization layer built
on top of the sweeping-path planner in Section 4.1, providing itGω that replacesG1 for simulation validation.
4.2.1 Objective
Given (m,Sm) and a randomly sampled building state Gω from G2 (analogous to a ground-truth G1), we
extract the evolving graph G2(t;Gω), task room set Rtask(t;Gω), and responder states (sk(t),tk) during the
sweep from Section 4.1. Running the planner until all rooms are cleared yields the random sweep cost

wherecresp ≥ 0 represents labor cost or responder scarcity and the expectation ofJresp is estimated via adaptive

where adding a responder costs more than the time saved.

number of responders m, we perform multiple Monte–Carlo trials following two steps: (a) selecting optimal

G1) from G2 and running a full sweep simulation using DynamicPlanner (Algorithm 1). The procedure is
described in Algorithm 5.

5 Results of Responders Reacting to a Fire Alarm
We apply our model to three building layouts of varying size and ﬂoor structure, performing sensitivity
analysis across random seeds to demonstrate robustness.
For each scenario, we visualize sweeping paths and responder conﬁgurations on the ﬂoor plans. Each
responder is assigned a unique color; circles mark starting positions and arrows indicate ﬁnal locations. Paths
appearing on multiple ﬂoors correspond to responders moving between levels via stair edges while completing

are brieﬂy shown in Appendix.
5.1 Layout 1: The Provided Floor Plan of an Oﬃce
As a proof of concept, we apply our sweep-path planner to the contest’s oﬃce layout (Fig. 5.1) using a ﬁxed
responder count m =2 and one sampled building stateGω (treated as G1). After applying our model, we are
able to obtain the total sweeping time, 77.1 seconds, with the ﬁrst responder sweeping for 69.6 seconds and
the second responder sweeping for 77.1 seconds. The resulting routes are shown in Fig. 5.2. The ﬂoor is cut in
half, with each responder clearing 3 rooms in order. This plan matches human intuition as to travel in indentical
routes in symmetric layouts.

For the hospital ward, our outer optimizer recommends a team of 4 responders. As shown in Fig. 5.5(a),
an increase ofm from 1 to 4 cuts the expected sweep time from about22 min (1300 s) to roughly5 min(300 s).
After having 4 responders, additional responders yield only small further reductions while steadily increasing the
total costJresp due to the per-responder penalty. The bar chart also shows that the bandit algorithm automatically
focuses its simulation budget on responder counts near this optimal value. The timelines in Fig. 5.5(b) conﬁrm
our result that with four responders the sweep ﬁnishes in about270 s. All responders stay busy and ﬁnish within
a narrow time window, indicating that the planner balances workload eﬀectively across the team.

5.2.Potential Host Cities That Have Never Hosted the Super Bowl 
Among cities that own NFL teams but have never hosted a Super Bowl, we selected 
three: Baltimore, Maryland; Seattle, Washington; and Denver, Colorado. 
We collected environmental impact data for these three cities. For data not publicly available 
or impossible to obtain—such as audience numbers and stadium specifications—we used the 
average values from the past five years of hosting events, ensuring general representativeness. 
Substituting each city's data into the Environmental Impact Assessment Model, we calculated 
the total scores using weights derived from the Entropy Weighting Method and the TOPSIS 
approach. The resulting environmental impact ratings for the Super Bowl across the three cities are 
presented in the table below: 
Table 5. Scores of three non-host cities. 
Cities R 
Baltimore, MD 0.3061 
Seattle, WA 0.4292 
Denver, CO 0.3385 
Figure 11. Specific scores of three non-host cities. 
Among the three new candidate cities, Seatt le, WA achieved the highest score of 0.4292, 
demonstrating strong potential as a Super Bowl host. Fig.11 presents the detailed scores for each city 
across Total Carbon Footprint, Ecological System Score , Air Quality, and Nuisance Impact. Seattle 
performs particularly well in carbon emissions, owing to a clean electricity mix: approximately 90% 
hydropower, resulting in a grid carbon intensity of 0.02 kgCO₂/kWh. Its robust public transit network 
supports over 20% of urban travel, reducing tran sportation emissions, while private vehicle use 
accounts for only 30%. Walking and cycling also contribute to clean travel modes. 
Seattle’s ecosystem score is high due to ex tensive green spaces and a well-developed 
environmental indicator system. Air quality is mode rate, affected occasionally by wildfires and 
elevated particulate matter (PM) levels. 
In contrast, Baltimore and Denv er lag in carbon emissions and ecosystem scores. Baltimore’s 
reliance on fossil fuels (>50%) increases emissions, while Denver’s limited ecosystem resilience and 
water scarcity constrain vegetation and ecological capacity. If integrated with the nineteen previously 
evaluated cities, Seattle would rank 5th–6th, Denver 8th–9th, and Baltimore 9th–10th. 
Applying the model to all nineteen past Super Bowl host cities and the three new candidates, 
the San Jose–Santa Clara–Stanford metropolitan area emerges as the most suitable host, ranking first 
with a score of 0.5965. Cities ranked 2nd–5th—Los Angeles–Inglewood–Pasadena, San 
Diego, Minneapolis, and Miami—also demonstrate strong sustainability potential. Among 
the new candidates, Seattle’s score of 0.4292 places it just behind these top five, highlighting its 
suitability to host a sustainable Super Bowl.

6.6 TimeAdjustmentMethods
Inprevioussections, weappliedtheGRAandFuzzyComprehensiveEvaluationmodelstomeasurethe
environmentalsustainabilityofSuperBowlhostcities. However,werecognizethatcitieshostingtheSuper
Bowlinearlierdecadesmightnaturallyobtainlowerscoresbecauseoftechnologicallimitationsandhistorical
constraints. Forinstance,renewableenergyadoptionwasminimalbeforethe2000s,wasterecyclingsystems
werelessdeveloped,andtransportationemissionsweresignificantlyhigher. Assustainabilitytechnologiesand
environmentalpolicieshaveadvancedovertime,thesetemporaleffectscanunfairlypenalizeearlyhostcities.
Toensurefairnessandremovethebiasintroducedbytime-dependenttechnologicalprogress,wedesigna
TimeAdjustmentMethodthatneutralizestheeffectofera-specificdevelopments.
6.6.1 RationaleandReferenceCitySelection
Ideally,wewouldcollectcompletehistoricaldataforallsustainabilityfactors(e.g.,energyefficiency,emis-
sions, waste rate) for each city from 1967 to 2025. However, such comprehensive datasets are not fully
available,especiallyforearlieryears. Toovercomethislimitation,weselectMiamiasthebenchmarkcityfor
temporaladjustmentbecause:
1. Miami has hosted the Super Bowl more than any other city, making it the most representative location
for observing changes in the event’s sustainability characteristics.
2. AsamajorU.S.metropolitanarea, Miamihasexperiencednearlyeverystageoftechnologicaladvancement
relevant to sustainability.
Thus,weusehistoricalcriteriadataofMiami(1967−2025)tomodelthetemporalevolutionofsustainability-
relatedfeatures,andthenremovethesetimetrendsfromthedataofallcandidatecities. Forevent-speciﬁc
criteria(e.g. Attendance/TicketSold),weuseinterpolationtoestimatevaluesforyearsinwhichtheSuper
BowlwasnothostedinMiami.

5 Problem2: Single-StoryOfficeSweepModel
Building on the key elements defined in Section 4, the core “Space–Risk–Decision” model
wasconstructedtoanalyzethebaselinescenarioinProblem2,inwhichtwofirefighterssweep
asingle-storyofficebuilding. Thephysicalstructureofthebuildingwasfirsttranslatedintoa
mathematicalframeworkcompatiblewithalgorithmicdecision-making.Thisrequiredthecreation
oftwofoundationalcomponents:agraph-basedrepresentationandadynamicriskmodel.
5.1 Graph-BasedBuildingRepresentation
The building ﬂoor plan is modeled as a dynamic, weighted, undirected graph,
G(t)=( V,E,W (t)).
This abstraction enables the application of rigorous, well-established mathematical methods for
routing and path optimization.
Graph Construction The graph is deﬁned through its primary components:
• Node Set V: The model uses four categories of nodes:
– VR: Room nodes (six oﬃces). Each room noder stores essential attributes:
Attr(r)= {ID, Area, (x,y),R0,VerifStatus}.
Here, R0 represents the static risk level (e.g., 0.8 for a standard oﬃce), and VerifStatus
tracks the room’s sweep progress (Unswept, Primary, Veriﬁed), supporting the redun-
dancy requirements.
– VC: Corridor nodes. The central hallway is discretized into six nodes (C1 ...C 6)t o
allow ﬁne-grained routing updates and smoke propagation modeling.
– VX: Exit nodes,{XWest,XEast}, which act as the initial positions for the two responders.
– VM: Veriﬁcation markers. For each high-risk room, virtual nodes are added to enforce
the required dual-sweep protocol.
• Edge Set E: Edges connect adjacent nodes according to architectural adjacency (hallway
segments, room–hallway transitions, and hallway–exit links).
Page 8 of 26
• Dynamic Edge WeightsW(t): The traversal cost for an edgee varies over time based on
length d(e) and the responder’s eﬀective speedveﬀ(e,t):
W(e,t)= d(e)
veﬀ(e,t) ·γ(e,t),
where γ(e,t) is a risk penalty multiplier that increases the cost of traversing hazardous or
low-visibility regions.
To ensure the model is reproducible, we developed a clearTopology Construction Algo-
rithm (Algorithm 1). This algorithm takes any ﬂoor plan as input and outputs the graphG,
which becomes the foundation for all subsequent routing.

2: Output:G r a p hG =( V,E,W 0)

5.2 Dynamic Risk and Hazard Modeling
In an emergency like a ﬁre, the environment is not static. A successful model must account for
spreading hazards. Therefore, our team integrated a dynamic risk module to simulate the spread
of smoke and its direct eﬀect on responders (inspired by risk evolution frameworks such as [8]).
Physics-Based Smoke Diﬀusion Our team employed a simpliﬁed advection-diﬀusion model
(Eq. 2) calibrated to NFPA 92 smoke control standards [2]. For a ﬁre source at nodev0 (assumed
at R1), the smoke concentrationS at any nodev evolves as:
S(v,t)= Smax ·(1−e−kt)·e−λh·dh(v,v0) (2)
where Smax is the max concentration, k is the ﬁre growth rate, and λh is a horizontal decay
coeﬃcient. This formula models the physical reality: the ﬁre grows over time (1− e−kt) but its
eﬀect lessens over distance (e−λhdh). We deﬁne any zone whereS(v,t) ≥ 0.05 kg/m3 as hazardous.
Page 9 of 26
Impact on Operational Eﬃciency Smoke and fatigue directly degrade a responders ability
to move and search. Which
• Movement Speed: We deﬁned eﬀective speedveﬀ as:
veﬀ(p,t)= vbase ·max{0.5,1−α·S(p,t)−ζ(t)} (3)
This formula includes a penaltyα for smoke S(p,t)a n dafatigue factor ζ(t)=0 .0005 ·
tcumulative [2]. This fatigue factor ensures that as the sweep continues, the responder’s speed
realistically degrades.
• Sweep Time:The time to sweep a room is not constant. It depends on the room’s baseline
diﬃculty and current hazards:
Tsweep(r,t)= Tbase(r)·[1+ β ·S(r,t)+ η · IHighRisk(r)] (4)
Here, Tbase is the base time (e.g., 30s for an oﬃce). We add a penaltyβ for smoke and
a complexity penalty η (e.g., 0.5) for high-risk areas like daycares, which require a 50%
longer inspection [2].
5.3 Sweep Optimization and Task Allocation
With the building represented as a risk-aware graphG(t), our core challenge was to solve the
task allocation problem: how to assign the 6 rooms (VR) to the 2 responders (N = 2) to minimize
the total time for thelast responder to ﬁnish (the ”makespan”) [9].
To solve this problem, the model begins with a formalProblem Formulation. The objective
is to determine the assignmentA = {A1,A2} that minimizes the makespan, subject to four critical
constraints:
min Tmakespan =m a x
j∈{1,2}
{Ttotal(j)} (5)
s.t. A1 ∪A2 = VR and A1 ∩A2 = ∅ (Complete Coverage) (6)
CV ≤ 0.15 (Load Balance) (7)
∀r ∈ VHighRisk
R : VerifStatus(r) = Veriﬁed (Redundancy) (8)
The load-balance constraint (Eq. 7)is evaluated using the coeﬃcient of variation (CV), deﬁned
as:
CV = σ(Ttotal)
μ(Ttotal) (9)
where σ(Ttotal)a n dμ(Ttotal) are the standard deviation and mean of all responders’ total work
times. We requireCV ≤ 0.15, ensuring no responder is overburdened.
Page 10 of 26
Heuristic Solution Algorithm Since this problem is NP-hard (similar to a multi-depot vehi-
cle routing problem), our team developed a high-performanceHeuristic Solution Algorithm
(Algorithm 2) that balances load and prioritizes risk. The algorithm operates in two phases:
1. Phase 1 (Primary Sweep): Rooms are sorted by priority (RiskR0 +S m o k eS(r,t)).
The algorithm iteratively assigns the highest-priority unswept room to the responder who
can reach and clear it thefastest (based on their currentTtotal), which actively promotes
load balancing. Pathﬁnding is done using a modiﬁed Dijkstra’s algorithm on the dynamic,
risk-weighted graph [9, 3].
2. Phase 2 (Redundancy): After Phase 1, the algorithm reviews all high-risk rooms. It
generates a new veriﬁcation task for each, assigning it to theother responder (the one who
did not do the primary sweep).
Algorithm 2 Prioritized Greedy Task Allocation with Redundancy
1: Input:G r a p hG, RespondersN = 2, Start positions{XW,XE}
2: Output: Assignments{A1,A2}, MakespanTmakespan
3: Initialize: Ttotal(j) ← 0, Aj ←∅ ,p o s (j) ← Startj
4: U ← VR ⊿Unassigned tasks
5: // Phase 1: Priority-based primary sweep
6: while U ⁄= ∅ do
7: Sort U by descending priority:R0(r)+ S(r,t)
8: r∗ ← highest priority room inU
9: j∗ ← argminj{Ttotal(j)+RiskPenalizedDijkstra(pos( j),r∗,t)}
10: Ttravel,Tsweep ← Calculate travel and sweep times forj∗ to r∗
11: Ttotal(j∗) ← Ttotal(j∗)+ Ttravel +Tsweep
12: Aj∗ ← Aj∗ ∪{r∗},p o s (j∗) ← r∗
13: U ← U \{r∗}, VerifStatus(r∗) ← Primary
14: end while
15: // Phase 2: Redundancy veriﬁcation sweep
16: for each r ∈ VHighRisk
R do
17: jprimary ← responder who did primary sweep ofr
18: jverify ← other responder
19: Ttravel,Tverify ← Calculate times forjverify to r
20: Ttotal(jverify) ← Ttotal(jverify)+ Ttravel +Tverify
21: Ajverify ← Ajverify ∪{rverify}, VerifStatus(r) ← Veriﬁed
22: end for
23: Return Tmakespan =m a x{Ttotal(1),Ttotal(2)}
The problem requires complete veriﬁcation of all rooms. For any high-risk space (e.g., a daycare
or laboratory), the system implements adual-sweep protocol: a primary sweep performed by
one responder, followed by a secondary veriﬁcation (a 15s re-check) conducted by the alternate
responder. As shown in later results, this protocol introduces a predictable time overhead but
Page 11 of 26
yields a 99.5% conﬁdence in room clearance—a trade-oﬀ that appropriately prioritizes occupant
safety.

lem statement. The building layout (Figure 3) contains six rooms and two exits. To evaluate the
risk-prioritization mechanism, Rooms R1 and R2 were designated as high-risk laboratories (risk
factor = 1.5), while Rooms R3–R6 were treated as standard oﬃces (risk factor = 0.8). The ﬁre
originates in Room R1, generating the dynamic smoke spread shown in Figure 2.

kg/m3).

the veriﬁcation of R1.

The simulation produced a total makespan of124.6 seconds. Detailed results in Table 3 show
that all design objectives were satisﬁed.
• Load Balancing: The two responders achieved nearly perfect workload distribution (R1:
114.6s, R2: 124.6s). This yields an exceptionally low coeﬃcient of variation (CV) of5.9%,
far below our 15% target threshold.
• Risk Prioritization: Both high-risk laboratories were swept ﬁrst. R1 was completed at
19.9 seconds, and R2 at 29.2 seconds. This represents a 60% time advantage over standard
oﬃce rooms.
• Redundancy Compliance: Both high-risk rooms received mandatory dual-sweep veriﬁca-
tion. R1 was re-veriﬁed by Responder 2 (109.6-124.6s), and R2 by Responder 1 (99.6-114.6s),
respecting the 60-second safety interval.
Page 13 of 26
• Safety Assurance: Maximum smoke exposure was 0.0713 kg/m3 (71.3% of IDLH thresh-
old), maintaining responder safety.
• Complete Coverage: All six rooms achieved 100% primary sweep completion, with 100%
redundancy veriﬁcation for high-risk areas.
Benchmark Comparison To validate the eﬀectiveness of our priority-based algorithm, our
team conducted a comprehensive comparison against three baseline strategies:
1. Random Assignment: Randomly assigns rooms with no logic.
2. Nearest-Neighbor Greedy: Each responder sequentially selects the closest unvisited
room.
3. Equal Partition: Statically assigns 3 rooms to each responder based on proximity.
As shown in Table 4, our priority-based algorithm achieved a43.9% time reduction (79
seconds saved) compared to the best-performing baseline strategy (Equal Partition at 203.6 sec-
onds). More critically, our algorithm is theonly method that guarantees both load balancing and
high-risk prioritization simultaneously.
Table 4: Algorithm Performance Comparison (10 Runs Each)
Strategy Mean ±Std(s) Best (s) Load CV Risk Priority?
Random Assignment 205.4 ± 38.0 158.1 24-35% Fails 60% of runs
Nearest-Neighbor 222.0 ± 6.0 216.5 18-22% Partial (no guarantee)
Equal Partition 209.6 ± 3.8 203.6 2-4% No (ignores risk)
Our Algorithm 124.6 ± 0 124.6 5.9% Yes (100% guaranteed)
Improvement vs. Best Baseline:43.9% faster(79 seconds saved)
Figure 4 compares solution quality and computation cost for the same small instance using
an exact solver (denoted “Optimal”) and our greedy allocation heuristic (“Greedy”). The exact
allocation achieves a substantially lower makespan (approximately 43s versus about 85s for the
greedy policy), at the cost of greater computation (roughly 18ms for the exact method versus
≈10ms for the greedy method). These results indicate that, for small instances or oﬄine planning,
exact optimization can meaningfully reduce evacuation time; conversely, in low-latency or online
settings the greedy heuristic remains attractive due to its much smaller runtime overhead.
Overall, analysis of the simulation provided several insights:
• Deterministic Stability: Our algorithm is deterministic; it produces zero variance (std =
0.0s) because its priority calculation is based on risk and real-time smoke, ensuring consistent
performance.
• Redundancy Eﬃciency: The 87.5% redundancy overhead is an acceptable trade-oﬀ as
it guarantees dual veriﬁcation of high-risk areas (R1, R2) without delaying the overall
makespan, thanks to the 60-second safety interval.
Page 14 of 26
Figure 4: Comparison between the exact solution (Optimal, brute-force/ILP) and the greedy
allocation (Greedy) on a small test instance. Left: total completion time (makespan). Right:
corresponding computation time.
• Smoke-Aware Routing: Room R1, despite being the ﬁrst priority, required 11.6 seconds
to sweep due to high smoke concentration (0.0713 kg/m3). This demonstrates the model’s
realistic incorporation of environmental hazards.
• Parallel Eﬃciency: Both responders operated continuously with minimal idle time. The
8.7% workload imbalance reﬂects an optimal task distribution given the asymmetric smoke
propagation.

7 SensitivityAnalysis
7.1 PurposeandMethodology
Aftertestingthemodel,wecontinuewithasensitivityanalysisofthemodel.
Weemployaone-factor-at-a-timeMonteCarlo(OFAT)approach[7],whereonlyoneparameterisvariedatatime
whileallothersremainattheirbaselinevaluestoassessthemodel’srobustness.Eachparameterisrandomly
sampled20timeswithinaspecifiedrange,andthemodeloutputismeasuredbythecompletiontime:𝑇=max𝑔𝑇𝑔
The impact of parameter changes, furthermore, is quantiﬁed using relative RMSE:
𝑅𝑀𝑆𝐸 =
√
1
𝑁
/summationtext.1𝑁
𝑖=1(𝑇𝑖 −𝑇𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒)2
𝑇𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒
· 100%
This normalized metric allows for comparison across parameters. Speciﬁcally, a higher RMSE indicates greater
sensitivity, while a lower RMSE suggests that the model is robust to variations in that parameter.

Figure 13: Radar graph of sensitivity analysis
The sensitivity analysis results are summarized as follows:
1. High sensitivity: 𝑡0,𝜂𝑔
2. Medium-high sensitivity: 𝜅𝑛

The model is most sensitive to𝑡0 and 𝜂𝑔, indicating that the baseline room workload estimates and personnel

4 StrengthsandLimitations
Havingdeveloped,applied,andtestedourmodel(Sections2–3),wenowcriticallyevaluate
itscapabilitiesandconstraints. Thisassessmentdemonstratesourunderstandingofhowour
modelingchoicesimpactthereliabilityandscopeofourconclusions.
4.1 Strengths
Ourmodeldemonstratesseveralkeystrengthsthatallowittobeadequatelyappliedtothe
problemathand. First,ourpathfindingmodelalignswiththesafetyandrescueprioritiesof
areal-worldsweep. Eachroomistobesearchedandvisitedatleastonce,ensuringthatthe
firefighting team can sweep the entire floor. In addition, we added time limit deadlines to
eachroominourextendedmodelbasedonthepointwherethefireoriginated. Thisallows
certainrooms to be prioritized and thoroughly searched before being consumed by the fire.
Moreover,our use of Ant Colony Optimization to solve the TSP problem and generate an
optimal path for multiple firefighters is both strong and time-efficient. Our algorithm is
versatile, meaning that one can use it to generate an optimal path for different situations
with different numbers of firefighters. Its efficiency is another significant advantage,
because in a dire situation likeevacuating occupants from an indoor fire, firefighters need
to know which path they need totraverseinabuilding,tominimizesweepingtime,assoon
aspossible.
Second,ourmodeltakesthedifferentconditionsofeachroomintoaccountwithourdifficulty
scoresderivedfromtheAnalyticHierarchyProcess. Weconsideredavarietyoffactorsthat
mightaffectthecleartimeofeachroom, includingbutnotlimitedtotheareaoftheroom,
itspotentialhazardlevel, andthenumberofoccupantsinit. Bydoingso, wecanderivea
relatively realistic clear time for each room based on its actual layout. This model is also
proventoberigorousbyourconsistencycheck,withaconsistencyratioof0.0242,whichis
lessthan
T eam # 16691 Page 20 of 26
0.1000. Therefore, it is a practical model that predicts the unique clear time required for each
room under speciﬁc conditions.
Our models are not just discrete mathematical formulas. Models 1 and 2 are jointly used
to achieve one purpose: to calculate an optimal path for ﬁreﬁghters under a realistic, indoor
ﬁre hazard. We add the clear time determined by Model 2 to the time used to travel from
one room to another (which is determined by the distance between two rooms divided by the
speed of a ﬁreﬁghter) to calculate the edge weight between two nodes in our graph (the nodes
represent the doors of each room). This value is used as the heuristic value in the ACO model,
which impacts road choice from one node to the next. This combination of two mathematical
models combines optimal road choice with real-life factors, grounding our completed model
with concrete empiricism.
Finally, the application of our model to diﬀerent building layouts reveals its robustness.
Not only can it operate under basic and ideal situations, but it also proves its worth in much
more complex scenarios. The versatility of our model is precisely why it is strong - it can
provide ﬁreﬁghters with a reasonable route plan in almost every circumstance, providing a valid
baseline for their sweep operation. Because it has a solid connection to real-life factors, a high
calculation eﬃciency, and applies to almost any case, our model provides a strong foundation
for designing an optimal path for indoor ﬁre sweeps in a limited amount of time.
4.2 Limitations
Despite these strengths, there is a need to acknowledge several limitations present in our
model. Assumption 3 is an overarching assumption that determines the movement rules of
ﬁreﬁghters. It dictates that ﬁreﬁghters will always take the shortest path from room to room,
which is an underlying ﬂaw in our method: if the ﬁre spreads to a point where the shortest path
is obstructed, the ﬁreﬁghters are then forced to take another path that allows them to circumvent
the ﬁre. Although we considered a ﬁre-spread model, we only used it to calculate a deadline
for each room to be all-cleared; we did not apply it to this speciﬁc case. Hence, this assumption
of ﬁreﬁghter movement reduces the model so that an accurate result might fail to present itself
in circumstances where the ﬁre spreads to the extent that the shortest path is obstructed.
Another limitation in our model arises because of Assumption 4. We assumed that all
occupants are able to reach the nearest exit all on their own. Therefore, the role of the occupant
is reduced to a factor in our diﬃculty-level rating of each room in model2.2, which correlates
to a speciﬁc clearing time under an exponential function ( 2.2). However, this is not the case
in every situation. Oftentimes, occupants like infants or elderly inhabitants might struggle to
reach the exit by themselves. If this is the case, the ﬁreﬁghters may have to escort them to the
exit, resulting in a necessary detour back to the nearest exit, and a decrease in ﬁreﬁghter speed
on the trip. This aﬀects the ﬁnal, optimized clear time as well as the pathﬁnding algorithm. By
failing to consider the potential need for responders to escort occupants to the nearest exits, our
model cannot accurately mirror reality when confronting this type of situation.
The ﬁnal limitation of our model is its generalizability. In the model, we focused intensively
on a speciﬁc type of indoor hazard - ﬁres. If the full model is to be applied to another hazard,
such as an earthquake, an extrapolation will occur, and the model will not yield valid data.
However, the pre-extended model provides some insight into areas of expertise outside its
scope. It consists of a room-rating model, and a routing model based on the distance between
rooms as well as the time it takes to clear the room (derived from the rating model). These two
models, once combined, can potentially serve as a foundation that can be adapted into more
T eam # 16691 Page 21 of 26
speciﬁcmodelstoaddressbuildingsweepmethodologiesofotherhazards, includingbutnot
limitedtoearthquakesandlandslides.
Toconclude,ourmodelislimitedintermsoftwopreliminaryassumptionsthatoversimplify
the problem. The full model is also constrained because it is only applicable to sweeping
during fire hazards, though the baseline can provide inspiration for more extensive models
correspondingtootherdisastersthatrequireanefficientandextensivesweep.
Team 16624 Page 18
4. Alignment with Sustainability Literature. The model consistently rewards cities with cleaner elec-
tricity grids and higher-quality transit systems. This pattern matches ﬁndings from environmental science
and sports sustainability reports, which emphasise decarbonised power and low-carbon mobility as key
levers for reducing event footprints. Seeing our results line up with those existing studies reassures us that
the model is picking up real environmental patterns rather than quirks of the dataset we happened to use.
Weaknesses
At the same time, several limitations became apparent during the modelling process, and they are important
for interpreting the results carefully:
1. Static Snapshot of Environmental Performance. Our evaluation uses current–year or recent data
as proxies for how cities would perform during a future event. In practice, infrastructure evolves quickly,
especially when cities are preparing to host mega–events. A city that is investing heavily in renewables or
transit upgrades may be undervalued relative to its near–future capacity.
2. Uncertainty in Mobility Estimates. Fan and team travel patterns are diﬃcult to predict precisely.
Assumptions about mode shares, the split between air and ground travel, and typical travel distances
introduce uncertainty into the SMHI calculations. While these assumptions are reasonable at an aggregate
level, they still add noise that could slightly shift rankings, especially in the middle of the table.
3. Limited Treatment of Behavioural Factors. Our framework focuses on infrastructure and city-level
conditions. Behavioural aspects—such as how many spectators actually sort their waste correctly, or how
pricing and policy inﬂuence fan travel choices—are only roughly approximated through average parameters,
and are not something our current version really captures in detail. In reality, these behavioural factors
can have meaningful eﬀects on the environmental outcome of a speciﬁc event, even in a city with strong
infrastructure.
Overall, the model oﬀers a rigorous and reasonably stable way to compare potential host cities, but its
outputs should be read together with qualitative knowledge about local policies, planned investments and
on-the-ground behavioural initiatives.
Future Work
For us, the current CRITIC–TOPSIS set-up feels like a good ﬁrst version: it already helps compare cities on
a common environmental basis, but it also makes clear where the next improvements could go. Our work
suggests several directions that we think are especially promising:
• Dynamic Modelling for Evolving Infrastructure.Instead of treating every indicator as ﬁxed, future
versions could incorporate temporal dynamics for planned sustainability upgrades, such as renewable
energy transitions and public transport expansions. This would allow the model to speak not only to
“how cities look now” but also to “how they are likely to look by the event year”.
• Integration of Behavioural and Socio-Economic Factors.Adding fan behaviour analytics—such as
travel mode choice models or estimates of waste-sorting compliance—would complement the current
infrastructure-focused approach with more human-centred sustainability insights. Socio-economic
variables could also help identify whether environmental burdens are distributed fairly within host
cities.
Team 16486 Page 25
10 Letter to the Local Emergency Planning Committee
Dear Members of the Local Emergency Planning Committee,
We are writing to share our evacuation sweep strategies developed through a mathematical model
applied to various building layouts: a one-story oﬃce, a ﬁve-ﬂoor school, and a single-family house.
By converting each ﬂoor plan into a network of nodes and edges, we optimized responder routes to
ensure rapid and safe sweeps. Our model uses a priority weight system, considering room function,
occupant vulnerability, proximity to hazards, and lingering probability. For example, rooms such as
nurseries and laboratories are prioritized higher than storage areas, ensuring that responders address
the most vulnerable spaces ﬁrst.
The following principles have been found to ensure eﬃcient sweeps across diﬀerent layouts. First,
we recommend node-based planning, where building ﬂoor plans are digitized into graphs, enabling
pre-assigned sweep routes that minimize time and maximize coverage. Second, we suggest assigning
teams to adjacent zones rather than isolated rooms. In smaller layouts, such as a one-story oﬃce, two
responders should start at the same entrance, each clear all rooms on either sides of the corridor, while
in multi-ﬂoor buildings, responders clear zones ﬂoor by ﬂoor.
Third, priority-based routing is key—rooms with higher risks, such as those near kitchens, storage,
or labs, should be cleared ﬁrst. Vulnerable rooms, such as nurseries and classrooms, should also be
prioritized. Redundancy is built into the plan, where high-priority rooms are checked by more than
one team if possible, minimizing the chance of oversight. Furthermore, we recommend maintaining
optimal team sizes: three responders work best for multi-ﬂoor schools, and adding extra responders
yields minimal beneﬁt beyond three teams.
For diﬀerent building types, we suggest speciﬁc strategies. In oﬃce buildings, we recommend two
teams per linear corridor, with a focus on visitor areas and rooms near hazard sources. Redundancy can
be achieved by passing through cleared rooms to ensure no one is missed. For schools and childcare
centers, we recommend three teams: one for lower ﬂoors, one for the central ﬂoor, and one for upper
ﬂoors. High-priority rooms, such as classrooms near labs and kitchens, should be cleared ﬁrst. For
residential buildings, particularly complex homes with basements, three teams are ideal: one for the
bedroom wing, one for the living room/basement, and one for the kitchen/garage. Critical areas, like
nurseries and bedrooms, should always be ﬁrst-priority zones.
In addition to these strategies, we suggest the use of modern technologies to improve sweep
eﬃciency. Room sensors for smoke, CO, and occupancy in high-risk rooms can help reduce redundancy
and speed up checks. Digital ﬂoor plans should be used to store pre-planned routes for quick reference
during an emergency. Finally, thermal cameras and indoor positioning systems should be used by
specialized teams assigned to high-risk areas, such as basements or labs.
In conclusion, our model shows that optimal evacuation sweeps rely on clear zone assignments,
prioritization of high-risk rooms, and a manageable number of teams, with built-in redundancy. We
hope these strategies, when adapted to local building inventories, will enhance emergency preparedness
and evacuation eﬃciency.
Respectfully submitted,
Team 16486

3.3 Two-Phase Solution Approach
We decompose the problem into two phases:
Phase 1: Room Assignment (Clustering). We assign rooms to responders to balance
workload while respecting spatial locality. This reduces the problem from considering all possible
room partitions to optimizing within predetermined clusters.
Phase 2: Path Optimization (Routing).For each responder’s assigned rooms, we ﬁnd the
shortest path that visits all rooms and returns to an exit.
This decomposition trades optimality for computational tractability. While the joint optimiza-
tion problem is NP-hard [6], our two-phase approach ﬁnds near-optimal solutions in reasonable
time.
3.4 Clustering Algorithm
Our greedy workload-balanced clustering algorithm assigns rooms to responders based on cur-
rent workload and proximity:
1. Initialize responder positions at distinct exits
2. Set workload Wi = 0 for all respondersi
3. While unassigned rooms remain:
(a) Select responder i∗ =a r gm i ni Wi with minimum workload
(b) Among unassigned rooms, selectr∗ =a r gm i nr d(r,posi) nearest to responderi∗
(c) Assign room r∗ to responder i∗
(d) Update workload: Wi∗ = Wi∗ + sr∗ + d(r∗,posi∗)
(e) Update position: posi∗ = r∗
Thedistanced(r1,r2)istheshortestpathtraveltimebetweennodes,computedusingDijkstra’s
algorithmonthebuildinggraph[3].
Thisgreedyapproachhastwokeyproperties: (1)itmaintainsspatiallocalitybyalwaysas-
signing the nearest room, reducing travel overhead, and (2) it balances workload dynamically,
preventingsituationswhereoneresponderisoverwhelmedwhileothersﬁnish.
3.5 PathOptimizationAlgorithm
Givenaresponder’sassignedrooms, weﬁndan e ﬃcienttraversal pat husi ngamulti-stage
travelingsalesmanproblem-inspiredsolver[2,5,7,10]:
Stage1: Nearest-NeighborInitialization.Startingfromtheresponder’sinitialexit,build
apathbyrepeatedlyvisitingthenearestunvisitedroom.Thisprovidesareasonableinitialsolution
quickly[10].
Stage 2: 2-Opt Local Search. Iterativelytestswappingpairsofedgesinthepath. If
swapping edges (ri,ri+1) and (rj,rj+1) with (ri,rj) and (ri+1,rj+1) reduces total path length,
accepttheswap.Repeatuntilnoimprovingswapsexist[7].
Stage3:Or-OptImprovement.Testrelocatingsequencesofonetothreeconsecutiverooms
tootherpositionsinthepath.Thisescapeslocalminimathat2-optcannotimprove[8].

Thiscombinationofnearest-neighborinitializationandlocalsearchimprovementstypically

2.3 Integrated Results and Model Application
Now that we have articulated our two basic models, a few questions arise, and we intend to
address them in this section. The problems are as follows:
• The algorithm we used to solve the md-mTSP is too ineﬃcient, because the time required
increases in a factorial way with every additional node. This makes it diﬃcult to compute
an optimal route for a complex building plan, because the brute force method is slow.
Therefore, we have to reﬁne our algorithm.
• At the moment, Models 2.1 and 2.2 are disjoint. We have to ﬁnd a way to link them
together in order to yield an optimal path.
• Our baseline model only applies to one speciﬁc scenario. We have to apply it to other
scenarios to ensure that it works as intended.
At the end of this section, these three questions will all be addressed through the processes of
model reﬁnement and application.
2.3.1 Model Reﬁnement
Our ﬁrst objective in model reﬁnement is to increase the eﬃciency of our model. To do this,
we will use Ant Colony Optimization (ACO) in order to solve the md-mTSP.

Figure 5: An Overview of Ant Colony Opti-
mization’s Workﬂow.
The basic idea of ACO is simple. Instead
of salesmen traveling from city to city, we use
colonies of ants. The ﬁrst ant will traverse all
must-pass cities by traveling from one city to the
next, nearest city, leaving a chemical trail called
a pheromone. This pheromone trail guides other
ants in the same colony to retrace the paths of the
previous ants [6].
However, the pheromone levels of a trail vary
based on its length. Ants only have so many
pheromone chemicals, so a longer path will have a
weaker pheromone level on its edges. In contrast,
a shorter path will have a lot more pheromones.
In ACO, there are multiple iterations of ants.
After the ﬁrst ant traverses all the cities, a second
ant will try and do the same thing, but instead of
doing it based entirely on heuristics like the ﬁrst
ant, the second ant has a choice to either follow the
strongest pheromone path or walk to the nearest
city, which is based on a heuristic value, which we
will explain later.
After many iterations, the ants will eventually
ﬁgure out a close approximation of the shortest
path that connects all the must-pass cities. This is
because each ant makes slight adjustments to the
paths that the previous ants take, until no adjust-
ment can be made, which is when the ants start
reinforcing the optimal solution through a positive
feedback loop: a stronger pheromone trail (that is
close to the optimal solution) attracts more ants,
and becomes even stronger for later iterations to follow.
This brief explanation above explains the fundamental idea of ACO, and we will proceed
by explaining how our reﬁned model deals with this method. The cities that the ants have to
traverse become the must-pass door nodes in our model. The presence of multiple ﬁreﬁghters
is the equivalent of multiple ant colonies with the shared purpose of visiting all door nodes at
least once.
Before we go any further, we have to deﬁne the pheromone and heuristic values. The
pheromone level of each path is initialized to the same value. Each iteration deposits a certain
amount of pheromones on the edges that are traversed by the ants. This amount is given by:
Q
Ttotal
whereQ is a constant indicating the total amount of pheromones an ant has, andTtotal refers to
the total time that the ant spent on the path in its iteration.
In addition, the pheromones will evaporate for all paths when an iteration ends. Each path
T eam # 16691 Page 14 of 26
receives a50% decrease in pheromone level, given by the equation:
τnew =( 1−ρ)·τoriginal
whereτ represents the pheromone level of each edge, andρ represents the evaporation constant,
which is set at0.50.
Therefore, the change in pheromone levels of an edge after an iteration, Δτ, depends on
whether the edge has been traversed in one iteration:
Δτ =

−(1−ρ)·τoriginal(foredgesnottraversed )
So, at the end of one iteration,
τnew = τoriginal +Δτ
for each edge. Next, we have to cover the heuristic valueη. It is calculated by the equation:
ηij = 1
cost(i,j)+ ϵ
where
• ηij represents the heuristic value of the edge between nodesi andj.
• cost(i,j) is the travel cost between the two nodes, which is the sum of the time needed to
travel from one node to the other and the time needed to clear roomj.
• ϵis a tiny constant to avoid division by zero. Here, we used the value of1×10−9.
Having deﬁned these two key variables, let us introduce the essential routing equation of our
model, which is the probability that ﬁreﬁghterk will travel from nodei to node j in their path
k (recall that we usedk to express the path that thek-th ﬁreﬁghter takes in Model2.1):
Pk
ij = [τij]α ·[ηij]β
∑
n∈unvisited[τin]α ·[ηin]β
Here,
• τij represents the pheromone level on the edge between nodesi andj.
• ηij represents the heuristic desirability of traveling fromi to j.
• α is the parameter that controls the inﬂuence of the pheromone. Here, we set it to 1.
• β is the parameter that controls the inﬂuence of the heuristic. Here, we set it to 3.
• unvisited represents the set of nodes that the ﬁreﬁghters have not visited yet.
Before our algorithm picks the next node to go to in one particular iteration, it calculates the
probability value based on the pheromone and heuristic levels of diﬀerent edges. Then, it picks
the node with the highest probability, and designates that to become the next node in its path.
T eam # 16691 Page 15 of 26
If the same path is computed for 10 consecutive iterations of ACO, we count that as the
optimal path.
The next task at hand is to transform our ACO to solve not only a Single Depot, Single
Salesman Problem, but an md-mTSP. We solve this by using node partitioning: we consider
every possible combination of nodes divided into k groups, and assign each group to each
ﬁreﬁghter to see which grouping method yields the least time. That becomes the optimal group.
The problem with this method is that it uses brute force, which is extremely ineﬃcient.
Therefore, we switched to a more optimal method, the Genetic Algorithm (GA).
The Genetic Algorithm assigns each must-pass node to one of the available paths. Each
assignment pattern is a chromosome, and its ﬁtness is determined by running ACO on every
path group and measuring the longest route. GA operators (selection, crossover, and mutation)
continually reﬁne these assignments by searching for partitions that minimize this maximum
path cost. Because each partition is evaluated independently, the GA can run in parallel,
drastically increasing its time eﬃciency.
2.3.2 Model Application
After we reﬁned our model by implementing the ACO algorithm and GA partitioning, we
now apply it to diﬀerent buildings to ensure that results are consistent across diﬀerent scenarios.
