Problem Chosen: A 2025 HiMCM
Team Control Number: 16603
Summary Sheet
A Three-Stage Optimize–then–Simulate Model
for Rapid Building Clearance Under Disaster
Conditions
Summary
Introduction: The rapid and complete sweep of a building during an emergency is a critical,
life-saving operation. However, emergency planners often lack the tools to create robust plans
that account for the chaotic, probabilistic nature of disasters. This paper presents a 3-stage
“Optimize–then–Simulate” hybrid model designed to ﬁnd the optimal responder assignment
(K) and estimate a realistic, 95th-percentile clearance time ( Tclear) by modeling real-world
physics and random failures.
Methodology: Our model consists of three components: (1) Zone Optimization using
K-Means to assign K responders to compact building zones; (2) Path Optimization via a
Traveling Salesperson Problem (TSP) heuristic to ﬁnd the shortest search path within each
zone; and (3) Stochastic Simulation (Monte Carlo + A*) to model clearance times with
probabilistic disruptions, dynamic rescues, and pathﬁnding adjustments.
Results: Our “Optimize K” analysis (1,000 runs per K) on a baseline oﬃce scenario
identiﬁed an optimal team size of K =3 . The ﬁnal 100,000-run simulation for K =3
yielded a 95th-percentile Tclear of 8.4 minutes, showing that the mean time (4.1 minutes)
is a dangerously optimistic metric. When applied to a high-risk School (Fire) scenario, the
95th-percentile Tclear increased by roughly 520% to 43.7 minutes, driven by a high probability
of multiple, time-consuming A* rescue trips.
Conclusion Recommendations: Our model is a robust tool: the 95th-percentile clear-
ance time, not the mean, is essential for planning. UseK =3 (trade-oﬀ of speed/eﬃciency).
Worst-case analysis beats averages.
Keywords: Emergency evacuation, Monte Carlo simulation, K-Means, TSP, A*, stochastic
modeling, risk analysis

1 Introduction and Problem Restatement
1.1 Problem Background and Motivation
During emergencies such as ﬁres or gas leaks, every second is critical to occupant safety.
When ﬁrst responders arrive on scene, they must work against uncertainty: they do not
know exactly where individuals remain in the building, which routes are accessible, or where
structural or debris blockages may lie. Responders quickly evaluate the most eﬃcient path-
way to search every room, continuously recalibrating their paths as new information on
obstacles is communicated to them.
These sweeps are diﬃcult because they are strongly aﬀected by uncontrollable factors
such as smoke density, collapsed ceilings, jammed doors, and debris-blocked corridors. All
of these contribute to the time it takes to reach and save someone in a room. It is therefore
essential to model these sweeps mathematically, so that responders and planners can make
informed decisions before disasters occur.
1.2 Detailed Problem Restatement and Interpretation
To declare “all clear” for emergency evacuations, several safety and procedural conditions
must be satisﬁed:
• Responders must visually conﬁrm that no persons remain in each room.
• They must perform an audible check, calling out and reporting “clear” on their radios,
especially when visibility is poor.
• They must conduct a physical inspection of all subareas in a room (corners, behind furni-
ture, under desks when appropriate).
• They must evaluate residual hazards, including smoke, gas, or structural debris that could
still endanger safety.
Only after conﬁrming both human and environmental safety can the area be considered
truly “all clear.” The room is then clearly marked and documented over radio to prevent
re-entry and redundant searches.
When responders encounter remaining individuals, those individuals must be assisted or
carried to safety and transferred to medical personnel. Natural randomness in disasters is
inevitable. A path that appears optimal in advance may suddenly be blocked by debris or
smoke. Responders then attempt to navigate around blocked paths, and in worst cases, may
be delayed enough that some occupants cannot be reached in time.
We assume all ﬁrst responders entering the building are equally healthy, fast, and strong,
having been rigorously trained under standard procedure. Other personnel remain on the
outer perimeter, supporting triage and treatment once victims are brought outside.
The nature of occupants also plays a critical role in sweep dynamics. Key factors include
occupant weight, the number of occupants per room, and the fraction of children or elderly.
Facilities with children, such as daycares or schools, require responders to search every corner
carefully and assist young individuals who may be hiding. To avoid misinterpreting injuries
and needs, responders assist all remaining occupants. Each setting demands a tailored
Team # 16603 Page 5 of 25
approach that accounts for room layout, hallway distances, occupant behavior, and building
density.
The type of emergency additionally inﬂuences sweep operations. Fires, earthquakes, and
other hazards create distinct conditions that aﬀect both responder and occupant behavior.
In ﬁres, heat, ﬂames, and smoke must be avoided, often forcing responders to prioritize acces-
sible rooms ﬁrst. Earthquakes can cause structural damage, block exits, and create unstable
environments, requiring sweeps that account for debris and alternative escape routes. Each
hazard modiﬁes occupant evacuation speed, choice of escape routes, and responder tactics.
Our model therefore explicitly distinguishes between ﬁre and earthquake scenarios.
1.3 Sweep Strategy
The model follows a ﬁrst-pass sweep strategy, also known as a level sweep or zone sweep.1 In
this approach, responders conduct an initial search from ﬂoor to approximately hip height
across a deﬁned zone. The ﬁrst pass focuses on quickly identifying obvious victims, hazards,
or immediate threats. By prioritizing speed and broad coverage over extremely detailed
inspection, this strategy ensures that critical areas are rapidly cleared and major dangers
are not overlooked during the initial sweep. Detailed, slower secondary searches can occur
later if needed; our model speciﬁcally optimizes this time-critical ﬁrst pass.
1.4 Model Pipeline Overview
Our model runs in three main stages:
1. K-Means clustering divides the ﬂoor plan into zones to assign each responder.
2. TSP routing determines the shortest possible route within each assigned zone.
3. Monte Carlo simulation with A* pathﬁndingaccounts for real-world uncertainty
such as blockages, smoke, varying assistance times, and diﬀerent occupant weights
to produce a full distribution of sweep times and to identify the optimal number of
responders K.
1FIRESCOPE ICS-500 and FEMA US&R guidance describe level and zone sweeps as standard ﬁrst-pass
tactics.
Team # 16603 Page 6 of 25
Figure 1: Model pipeline ﬂowchart illustrating K-Means zoning, TSP routing, and Monte
Carlo + A* simulation.
2 Preparation and Assumptions
2.1 Modeling Assumptions
To build a realistic yet tractable model for determining “all clear” times, we adopt the
following assumptions:
A1: All mobile occupants have already evacuated: Sweeps occur after initial evacua-
tion; only those requiring assistance (children, elderly, injured, or mobility-limited) remain.
A2: All ﬁrst responders have the same physical capacities: Standardized training
means baseline speed, endurance, and strength are uniform.
A3: All remaining occupants require assistance: Anyone inside is unable to evacuate
unaided; responders must assist or carry them.
A4: Carrying an occupant reduces responder speed proportionally to occupant
weight: Speed decreases with occupant’s mass, based on anthropometric data.
A5: Communication among responders is eﬀectively perfect: Radio systems (ER-
RCS) are reliable; delays are incorporated as a variable only for blockages.
A6: Visibility penalties apply only to ﬁre scenarios:Only ﬁres generate enough smoke
to reduce visibility; earthquakes do not.
A7: No re-entry into cleared rooms: Once searched and marked, rooms are not re-
entered, avoiding redundancy per best-practice protocols.
A8: All rescues initiated are successful: If a path exists, responders can transport
found occupants; medical mortality and collapse are out of scope.
A9: Maximum of two responders per exit: Limits congestion at exits, following safety
protocols.
A10: Responders always use stairs, not elevators:Standard emergency protocol man-
dates stairs are used only.
Team # 16603 Page 7 of 25
A11: Room search is deliberate and slow; fatigue during search is negligible:
Search occurs at half normal speed; additional in-room fatigue is insigniﬁcant.
A12: Building geometry is accurately known: Floor plans are correct and known
pre-event for conversion into graph structures.
2.2 Research Data Supporting the Model
Baseline First Responder Speed
A study of wilderness paramedic candidates carrying approximately 20.4 kg of load on ﬂat
terrain reported an average walking speed of 7.6 km/h, or about 2.1 m/s. This value serves
as our baseline horizontal speedv0 for earthquake scenarios (ﬂat indoor surfaces with equip-
ment).For ﬁre scenarios, responders move faster to outrun ﬁre progression and reach victims
sooner. We increase v0 by 0.5 m/s, giving v0 =2 .6 m/s for ﬁres.
Fatigue Constant
Fatigue reduces movement speed over time. In The eﬀect of fatigue and training status on
ﬁreﬁghter performance, trained ﬁreﬁghters performed a simulated ﬁre-ground test (SFGT)
at baseline and after a single fatiguing exercise. 2 The baseline mean completion time was
approximately tbaseline ≈ 365.0 s, and the fatigued completion time increased by 9.6% given
by: tfatigued ≈ 365.0 × 1.096 ≈ 400.0 s.Assuming a ﬁxed normalized distance d =1 m, the
eﬀective speeds would be
vbaseline = 1
365.0 ≈ 0.00274 m/s,v fatigued = 1
400.0 ≈ 0.00250 m/s.
If speed decays approximately linearly, we writev(t)= v0 −ft, and solve forf over a repre-
sentative operation duration (e.g., 35 minutes). This yields a fatigue deceleration constant
on the order of f ≈ 7.06×10−6 m/s2, which we adopt for our simulation.
Impact of Visibility on Search Time
To quantify the impact of smoke on search eﬃciency, we rely on ﬁre safety benchmarks from
IAFSS and NIST. Optical density at which photoelectric smoke detectors activate is about
0.14 OD/m. Using Beer–Lambert: kvis = OD × ln(10) = 0.14 × 2.302 ≈ 0.322 m−1 Jin’s
visibility law gives S = C
kvis
, where C depends on target luminance. Using C =3 (light-
reﬂecting surfaces), we obtain S ≈ 3
0.322 ≈ 9.3 m. Jin’s experiments show human walking
speed declines when visibility drops below about 10 m. Our 9.3 m baseline lies directly in
this regime, motivating a variable speed model where: veﬀ(t)= kvis(t)·v(t),with kvis(t) ≤ 1
derived from smoke density evolution.
2Shepherd & Billaut (2019), summarized in the references.
Team # 16603 Page 8 of 25
Probability of Finding an Occupant
Post-disaster human behavior studies (e.g., Tohoku earthquake evacuation) show that “milling”
behavior often delays departure by up to 10 minutes for a substantial portion of occupants.
Median departure times between 3 and 28 minutes imply that at 10 minutes—roughly when
responders begin sweeps—between 25% and 50% of people may still be inside. We therefore
choose Pocc ∈ [0.20,0.30] as a realistic range for the probability that a given room contains
at least one remaining occupant, with building-speciﬁc values in Section 4.
Probability that a Path is Blocked
FEMA estimates that non-structural damage causing collapsed ceilings, fallen cabinets, or
blocked doors occurs in roughly 10–15% of buildings after strong earthquakes, with higher
rates in structures that are not up to code. We model individual path/blockage encounter
probability as Pblock =0 .15 for earthquake scenarios and Pblock =0 for ﬁre-only scenarios.
Weight Distribution and Carrying Capacity
Remaining occupants are assumed to require assistance. Occupant weight Wocc strongly
aﬀects carrying speed. With a 50:50 male–female split for adults and demographic weight
distributions, we sample adult and child distributions from CDC data to provide a basis for
estaimating how occupant weight may reduce a responder’s eﬀective speed.
Table 1: US Weight distributions of Adults and Children by Sex (Aug 2021–Aug 2023)
Female Mean Female SD Male Mean Male SD
Adults (18+) 80.8 20.6 90.3 20.9
Children (2–18) 43.5 20.6 47.1 24.6
Fireﬁghter deadlift capacity for intermediate-level male ﬁreﬁghters is about 145.3 kg; we
conservatively cap carrying capacity at 130 kg to accommodate movement. If Wocc exceeds
this bound, we treat the scenario as requiring alternate assistance (beyond our model’s scope)
and do not simulate such carries.
Door Opening Time
Under normal conditions, door opening time is short. 10 physical trials produced the sample
Topen = {2.35,2.28,2.33,1.91,1.71,2.02,2.14,2.29,2.08,2.15} s.
We use their mean and standard deviation for non-jammed doors. For jammed doors, we
adopt a capped exponential model with maximum 60 s, consistent with forcible-entry training
benchmarks: most doors open quickly, a few are slow, but extremely long attempts are
unrealistic.
Team # 16603 Page 9 of 25
Communication Time
Per FIRESCOPE ICS-500, responders must report unit identiﬁcation, swept area, occupant
status, hazards, actions taken, and resource needs. When a room is clear and hazard-free,
this can be communicated on the move with negligible additional time. When a blockage
is encountered, a short radio report is required. We model this as a uniform random delay
applied only when paths are blocked, given byTcomm ∼ Uniform(1,3) s.
Room Marking Time
Our team performed 10 trials of door-marking following FEMA National US&R guidance
(slash-then-X marking). The recorded times (s) were:
Tmark = {18.67,18.32,18.23,17.54,18.12,18.34,18.01,18.14,18.59,17.78},
.
Figure 2: Standard FEMA US&R marking scheme for room search status.
Vertical Travel Speed and Floor Height
The International Building Code speciﬁes stair riser heights between 4 in and 7 in; we adopt
0.178 m as a representative step height. Fireﬁghters in breathing apparatus ascend at about
0.94 steps/s and descend at 0.97 steps/s, so we use an average of 0.955 steps/s. Multiplying
by step height yields a vertical speed vbase,vert ≈ 0.170 m/s. Average ﬂoor-to-ceiling height
is about 2.29 m for residential buildings and 3.05 m for commercial/school buildings. We
use 3.05 m for the oﬃce, café, and school scenarios. Fire suppression benchmarks show that
90% of structure ﬁres are controlled within 11 minutes. We useTﬁre =1 1min as the cap on
active smoke generation.

3 Model I: Baseline Sweep Time Model
To address the complex dynamics of an evacuation sweep, our model is separated into three
computational stages: K-Means clustering, TSP routing, and Monte Carlo simulation with
A* pathﬁnding. These stages build on a set of core equations describing the physics and
time costs of responder actions.
3.1 Stage 0: Core Equations (Physics Engine)
3.1.1 Total Sweep Time
For a single responder k, the total sweep time is modeled as
Tk = Ttravel,k +Tsearch,k +Tassist,k +Tdoor,k +Tmark,k +Tcomm,k, (1)
where each term aggregates the contributions from all relevant events (movement, searches,
rescues, doors, marking, and communication).

In a multi-responder simulation, the building clearance time for one run is

3.1.8 Communication and Marking Times
Communication time is Tcomm ∼ Uniform(1,3) s, applied when a blockage is reported, and
is sampled from the uniform distribution between its observed min and max.
3.2 Stage 1: K-Means Clustering (Assigning Zones)
To assign zones, we represent the building as a graph G =( N,E ) where nodes N are room
centers and key intersections, and edgesE represent traversable connections with associated
distances. Node coordinates (xi,yi) are used to cluster rooms into K zones. K-mean solves:
min
μ1,...,μK
K∑
k=1
∑
xi∈Zk
‖xi −μk‖2 (12)
to produce compact, geographically coherent zones. This yields balanced workloads and
reduced travel distances.
3.3 Stage 2: Ideal Path Optimization (TSP)
Within each cluster Zk, we solve a TSP variant: ﬁnd a path that starts at the responder’s
exit, visits all rooms in Zk exactly once, and minimizes travel distance. For K =1 we
use a nearest-neighbor heuristic as a baseline; for K> 1 we use a Lin–Kernighan heuristic
to approximate the optimal tour. This produces an “ideal” sweep path for each responder,
ignoring stochastic disruptions.
Team # 16603 Page 14 of 25
3.4 Stage 3: Monte Carlo Simulation
To model real-world uncertainty, we simulate K responders following their TSP paths in
parallel over NMC runs. In each run we randomize: room occupancies ( Pocc, Nocc), path
blockages (Pblock), door states and times (Topen), occupant weights (Wocc), assistance times
(Tassist), marking times (Tmark).
For each run, we computeTk and Tclear =m a xk Tk. Repeating over many runs produces
an empirical distribution of clearance times from which we estimate mean, median, and
95th-percentile values.
3.5 A* Pathﬁnding in the Simulation
Responders do not blindly follow precomputed TSP routes. When unexpected conditions
arise, they use A* pathﬁnding.
3.5.1 Trigger 1: Path Blockage
When traversing an edge (u,v), a Bernoulli trial with probability Pblock determines if the
path is blocked (earthquake scenarios). If blocked, the edge is removed from G,a n dA *
computes a shortest alternative path from u to v on the updated graph. The travel time
along this new path is added to Tk.
3.5.2 Trigger 2: Evacuee Transport
When an occupant is found, the responder pauses the TSP route and uses A* to ﬁnd the
shortest path to their assigned exit. The cost function uses vcarry while carrying and veﬀ for
the return. The computed travel time plus Tassist are added to Tk.
3.6 Optimize Number of Responders ( K)
Increasing K decreases the load per responder but eventually yields diminishing returns. To
ﬁnd the optimal K,w e :
1. Run low-ﬁdelity Monte Carlo simulations (1,000 runs) for K =1 ,2,...,K max.
2. For each K, compute T95(K), the 95th-percentile clearance time.
3. Plot T95(K) vs. K and identify point at which adding responders has negligible im-
provement. Thusm the K at the elbow of this plot is selected as the optimal staﬃng
level.
3.7 Final High-Fidelity Simulation
After choosing Kopt, we run a high-ﬁdelity Monte Carlo simulation with NMC = 100,000
runs. This provides a robust estimate of the full clearance-time distribution for the optimal
staﬃng level, including rare but critical tail events.
Team # 16603 Page 15 of 25
4 Application of Model: Scenario Deﬁnitions
We apply the model to three building types (Oﬃce, Café, School) and two hazard types
(Earthquake, Fire), yielding six scenarios.
4.1 Oﬃce Scenario
Our ﬁrst scenario is a typical oﬃce environment with rectangular rooms, corridors, and a
moderate number of occupants (primarily adults). Nearly all occupants are working-age
adults; children are extremely rare. Based on Commercial Buildings Energy Consumption
Survey data, we approximate the probability of ﬁnding an odult occupant as 99.5% and the
probability of ﬁnding a child occupant as 0.5%.
Figure 3: Oﬃce ﬂoor plan used in the model.
4.2 Café Scenario
The second scenario is a café, with a mix of seating, service counters, and circulation space.
To estimate adult/child distribution, we observed a local café with 24 people: 3 workers,
18 adults, and 3 children. Ignoring our own observers, this corresponds to approximately:
we approximate the probability of ﬁnding an odult occupant as 90% and the probability of
ﬁnding a child occupant as 10%.
Figure 4: Café ﬂoor plan (ﬁrst and second ﬂoors).
4.3 School Scenario
The ﬁnal scenario is a multi-ﬂoor school environment with classrooms, hallways, and stair-
wells. A teacher-to-student ratio of approximately 1:16 implies that the probability of an
occupant being a child is 94% while the probability for adults is 6%.

6 Performance Evaluation and Validation
To ensure that our model accurately reﬂects real-world evacuation-sweep dynamics, we con-
ducted a series of validation and performance tests. These included benchmark comparisons,
internal consistency checks, and sensitivity analyses on key probabilistic parameters. All val-
idation procedures were performed after the 100,000-run high-ﬁdelity simulation using the
optimal responder count K =3 .
6.1 Benchmark Validation
We compared our predicted sweep times against empirical ranges published by the U.S. Fire
Administration, FEMA, and NIST. Fire response records indicate that medium-sized, multi-
ﬂoor structures typically require 20–45 minutes to reach an “all clear” conclusion under
ﬁre conditions. Our School–Fire scenario produced: Tmean =1 8.4 min,T 95 =4 3.7 min,
which falls directly within this nationally reported range. This alignment indicates that
our combination of visibility reductions, occupant rescues, and door-jam stochasticity is a
faithful representation of real sweeps.
6.2 Internal Model Validation
Internal checks were conducted by disabling one stochastic component at a time. When
blockages, occupants, and door randomness were all removed, the total sweep time reduced
to: Tclear = LTSP
v0
, which matched analytically predicted TSP-only traversal times. Similarly,
doubling the area of every room doubled all computed search times, conﬁrming the linearity
embedded in the search-time formulation.
6.3 Sensitivity to Blockage Probability
We varied the blockage probability Pblock ∈ [0,0.30]. Clearance times increased approxi-
mately linearly with blockage rate: T95(P) ≈ T95(0)+34 .2P min,showing that earthquakes
(where Pblock =0 .15) signiﬁcantly degrade sweep eﬃciency due to repeated A* detours.
Team # 16603 Page 22 of 25
6.4 Sensitivity to Occupant Probability
To model diﬀerent population loads, we varied Pocc ∈ [0.05,0.40]. Results show: dT95
dPocc
≈
57 min per unit, revealing that occupant density is the most inﬂuential variable driving
worst-case sweep times (especially in the School scenarios, where 94% of occupants are
children).
6.5 Sensitivity to Fatigue Constant
Adjusting the fatigue constant f by ±50% altered T95 by only 3–6%. Visibility reduction
(ﬁre) and blockages (earthquake) dominate the eﬀect of fatigue, which remains a secondary
factor.
6.6 Summary
The model displays strong internal consistency, matches national evacuation-sweep ranges,
and demonstrates logical sensitivity behavior. This conﬁrms its reliability as both a predic-
tive and comparative tool.
7 Model Strengths and Limitations
7.1 Strengths
• Hybrid Optimization–Simulation Pipeline: Combines K-Means zoning, TSP path
optimization, Monte Carlo simulation, and A* pathﬁnding to balance theoretical eﬃciency
with real-world uncertainty.
• Physics-Based Time Modeling: Fatigue, visibility, weight-dependent carrying speed,
door-jam statistics, and blockages use published research or empirical measurement.
• Scenario Flexibility: Disaster type toggles visibility penalties and blockage probabilities
to evaluate both ﬁres and earthquakes under one framework.
• Statistical Reliability: 100,000 Monte Carlo runs ensure upper-tail stability (95th per-
centile), essential for emergency planning.
7.2 Limitations
• Lack of Dynamic Fire Spread:Uses a smoke-detector activation threshold (0.322 m−1)
rather than full CFD-based dynamic smoke.
• Single-Occupant Rescue: Responders carry only one person at a time; multi-casualty
extractions not modeled.
• Static Blockage Conditions: Earthquake debris is assumed ﬁxed; aftershocks and on-
going collapse are not simulated.
• Uniform Responder Capability: All responders have identical physical characteristics;
natural variation not captured.
These limitations reﬂect standard HiMCM modeling scope, but they should be considered
when interpreting results.
Team # 16603 Page 23 of 25
8 Conclusion
We developed a three-stage model—K-Means clustering, TSP ideal-route optimization, and
high-ﬁdelity Monte Carlo simulation with A* detours—to estimate realistic sweep times
across six building–hazard scenarios.
Our ﬁndings demonstrate:
1. The 95th-percentile clearance time is essential. Mean times are misleading,
underestimating true sweep requirements by 80–300%.
2. The optimal responder count is K =3 . Additional responders beyond three add
minimal value due to zone fragmentation.
3. Schools yield the longest sweep times.The 94% child population leads to frequent
rescues and weight-penalized carries.
4. Earthquakes produce more variability than ﬁres. Blockages drive longer and
more uncertain sweeps than visibility penalties alone.
Overall, our model provides a data-driven framework for emergency coordinators, empha-
sizing worst-case reliability over optimistic averages. The resulting recommendations form a
robust operational guideline for evacuation planning.
Team # 16603 Page 24 of 25
9 Letter to Local Emergency Coordination Personnel (LECP)
To: Local Emergency Coordination Personnel (LECP)
From: Team #16603
Subject: Data-Driven Recommendations for Building Evacuation Sweeps
Dear LECP,
Our team developed a comprehensive simulation model to analyze building sweep opera-
tions under ﬁre and earthquake conditions. Unlike traditional planning methods that rely on
average clearance times, our approach generates a full probability distribution from 100,000
simulated evacuations for each scenario.
Across all building types, we found that the optimal deployment strategy uses three
ﬁrst responders. Increasing the team beyond three does not signiﬁcantly reduce clearance
time and may contribute to operational ineﬃciency.
Most importantly, we discovered that the 95th-percentile clearance time —not the
mean—is the correct safety metric for emergency planning. For example, in school ﬁre
scenarios:
Tmean =1 8.4 min,T 95 =4 3.7 min.
Planning for the mean value would underestimate the necessary clearance time by more
than 25 minutes, potentially jeopardizing lives.
We therefore recommend that:
1. Emergency sweep protocols adopt the 95th-percentile clearance time as the planning
standard.
2. At least three responders be deployed for medium/large multi-ﬂoor structures.
3. Drills and training exercises incorporate visibility reduction, structural blockages, and
occupant-carrying behavior.
4. Building layouts be pre-processed using clustering and TSP-based optimization to as-
sign eﬃcient search zones.
Our model is modular and can be tailored to individual facilities. We would be happy to
assist with implementation or building-speciﬁc simulation analysis.
Sincerely,
Team #16603

AI Utilization Report
To maintain transparency in accordance with COMAP policy, our team provides the fol-
lowing description of how AI tools were used during the preparation of this paper. All
mathematical modeling, simulation code, analytical reasoning, algorithm design, statistical
interpretation, and ﬁnal decision-making were performed entirely by the student team.
Use of AI Tools. Artiﬁcial intelligence tools (including ChatGPT and similar LLMs) were
used in limited and strictly non-analytical capacities:
• Language Editing and Grammar Review: We used AI tools to improve clar-
ity, remove redundancy, and reﬁne grammar in drafted paragraphs. No AI-generated
reasoning, results, or explanations were inserted into the technical modeling sections.
• LaTeX Formatting Assistance: AI tools were used to help format tables, ﬁgures,
section headers, and reference formatting in L ATEX. This included resolving syntax
issues, building consistent table environments, and correcting structural errors in long
documents.
• APA/Reference Formatting: AI was used to convert our manually collected cita-
tions into properly formatted APA entries. All bibliographic sources themselves were
selected, retrieved, and veriﬁed by the student team.
• Non-Technical Clariﬁcations: AI tools were used for phrasing suggestions in sec-
tions such as the introduction, conclusion, and the LEPC letter. At no point did AI
generate model assumptions, equations, parameter selections, or simulation outcomes.
• Figure Caption Support: AI was used solely to rephrase caption text to improve
readability. All ﬁgures themselves—including computational outputs, TSP visualiza-
tions, K-Means plots, and histograms—were generated entirely by the team.
What AI Did Not Do. To comply fully with competition rules, AI tools were not used
for:
• generating or selecting assumptions,
• designing or implementing any part of the mathematical model,
• creating, debugging, or running simulation or optimization code,
• performing calculations, data analysis, or computations,
• interpreting simulation results or producing conclusions,
• producing any ﬁgure, graph, or model output.
Team # 16603 Page 27 of 25
Team Responsibility. All modeling decisions—including the construction of the physics
engine, derivation of equations, implementation of fatigue and visibility models, simulation
design, A* integration, K-Means/TSP optimization, statistical evaluation, and all sensitivity
analyses—are solely the work of the student team. All interpretations, explanations, and
ﬁnal recommendations were written independently by the students.
This report accurately reﬂects the extent of AI involvement in the creation of this submission.

3.2 Stage1: K-MeansClustering(AssigningZones)
Toassignzones,werepresentthebuildingasagraph G=(N,E)wherenodes N areroom
centersandkeyintersections,andedgesErepresenttraversableconnectionswithassociated
distances. Nodecoordinates(xi,yi)areusedtoclusterroomsintoKzones. K-meansolves:
min
μ1,...,μK
K∑
k=1
∑
xi∈Zk
‖xi − μk‖2 (12)
to produce compact, geographically coherent zones. This yields balanced workloads and
reduced travel distances.
3.3 Stage 2: Ideal Path Optimization (TSP)
Within each cluster Zk, we solve a TSP variant: ﬁnd a path that starts at the responder’s
exit, visits all rooms in Zk exactly once, and minimizes travel distance. For K =1 we
use a nearest-neighbor heuristic as a baseline; for K> 1 we use a Lin–Kernighan heuristic
to approximate the optimal tour. This produces an “ideal” sweep path for each responder,
ignoring stochastic disruptions.
3.4 Stage3: MonteCarloSimulation
To model real-world uncertainty, we simulate K responders following their TSP paths in
parallel over NMC runs. In each run we randomize: room occupancies (Pocc, Nocc), path
blockages(Pblock),doorstatesandtimes(Topen),occupantweights(Wocc),assistancetimes
(Tassist),markingtimes(Tmark).
Foreachrun,wecompute Tk and Tclear =maxk Tk. Repeatingovermanyrunsproduces
an empirical distribution of clearance times from which we estimate mean, median, and
95th-percentilevalues.
3.5 A*Pathﬁndingin the Simulation
Responders do not blindly follow precomputed TSP routes. When unexpected conditions
arise,theyuseA*pathﬁnding.
3.5.1 Trigger1: PathBlockage
When traversing an edge(u,v), a Bernoulli trial with probability Pblock determines if the
path is blocked (earthquake scenarios). If blocked, the edge is removed from G, and A*
computes a shortest alternative path from u to v on the updated graph. The travel time
alongthisnewpathisaddedtoTk.
3.5.2 Trigger2: EvacueeTransport
When an occupant is found, the responder pauses the TSP route and uses A* to find the
shortestpathtotheirassignedexit. Thecostfunctionuses vcarry whilecarryingand veﬀ for
thereturn. Thecomputedtraveltimeplus Tassist areaddedtoTk.

4 Methodology
We now present our two-stage machine learning framework for evaluating environmental suitability.
The key idea is to let the data reveal natural environmental archetypes (Stage I) and then learn a
smooth mapping from environmental features to suitability scores (Stage II).
4.1 Overall Pipeline
The workﬂow can be summarized as:
Raw data
clean & impute
−−−−−−−−−−−−→ 𝑋𝑐,𝑡
standardize
−−−−−−−−−→ 𝑋′
𝑐,𝑡
GMM clustering
−−−−−−−−−−−−−→ 𝑌𝑐,𝑡
city aggregation
−−−−−−−−−−−−−→ 𝑌𝑐
ridge regression
−−−−−−−−−−−−→ ˆ𝑌𝑐.
4.2 Stage I: Unsupervised Environmental Archetypes
4.2.1 Gaussian Mixture Model
We model the distribution of standardized features𝑋′

4.2.2 Cluster Labeling and City-Level Scores
Each cluster𝑘 is characterized by its centroid𝜇𝑘 in the space of key environmental variables, such
as renewable fraction, drought percentage, PM2.5, and mean temperature. We interpret the clusters
as High-, Moderate-, and Low-Suitability archetypes and assign cluster-level labels𝑌𝑘 ∈{ 0, 0.5, 1}
accordingly.
A city–year observation gets a soft label𝑌𝑐,𝑡 based on its cluster. City-level labels are then
computed by averaging across time:
𝑌𝑐 = 1
𝑇𝑐
/summationdisplay.1
𝑡
𝑌𝑐,𝑡 ,
yielding a long-term suitability score for each city based purely on its cluster membership history.
4.3 Stage II: Supervised Learning via Ridge Regression
Given city-level feature vectors and labels(𝑋𝑐,𝑌𝑐), we learn a smooth mapping from environmental
characteristics to suitability scores using ridge regression with polynomial feature expansion.
4.3.1 Feature Map
Because many environmental relationships are nonlinear, we apply a second-degree polynomial
feature map:
𝜙(𝑥) = [𝑥1,...,𝑥 𝑑,𝑥 𝑖𝑥𝑗,𝑥 2
𝑖 ]1≤𝑖≤𝑗≤𝑑,
resulting in𝐷 transformed features (about 170 in our implementation).

The resulting table provides a compact numerical representation of each city’s environmental
performance. In Section 4, we will ﬁrst interpret the scores directly to rank candidate host cities and
then feed them into three simple machine-learning models (logistic regression, random forest, and a
small neural network) to estimate𝑝𝑐 = P(𝑦𝑐 = 1 | s𝑐) and choose an optimal host city.
4 Model Development
In Sections 1–3 we deﬁned six dimension-wise environmental cost scores for each candidate host city
𝑐,
s𝑐 = {𝑠𝑐,travel,𝑠 𝑐,energy,𝑠 𝑐,waste,𝑠 𝑐,water,𝑠 𝑐,mobility,𝑠 𝑐,climate
}∈[ 0, 1]6,
and an equal-weight composite index
𝑆equal

/summationdisplay.1

𝑠𝑐,𝑑, D = {travel, energy, waste, water, mobility, climate},
where smaller values indicate better environmental performance.
To support the 2029 Super Bowl decision, we now build three simple but complementary machine-
learning models:
• a logistic-regression classiﬁer;
• a random-forest (RF) classiﬁer;
• a small feed-forward (back-propagation) neural network (NN).
Each model takes the six-dimensional cost vector s𝑐 as input and outputs a probability that city 𝑐
is an environmentally “good” host. We then average the three probabilities to obtain a more robust
ensemble prediction.
4.1 City sets and labels
The NFL has used 19 diﬀerent host cities across the history of the Super Bowl. Since the league began
formally integrating sustainability metrics and environmental assessments into the event planning
process, only a subset of those cities have hosted:
• Recent hosts with formal sustainability reporting: Inglewood (California), Glendale (Ari-
zona), Las Vegas (Nevada), New Orleans (Louisiana).
Team 16911 Page 12
• Conﬁrmed future hosts under the same sustainability framework: Santa Clara (California)
and Atlanta (Georgia).
• Never-host cities with NFL teams:we focus on three representative candidates—Seattle (Wash-
ington), Denver (Colorado), and Nashville (Tennessee)—which are often discussed in the media
as plausible future Super Bowl hosts because of their stadium quality and fan base.
Our core NFL city dataset thus contains nine U.S. cities:
CNFL = {Inglewood, Glendale, LasVegas, NewOrleans, SantaClara, Atlanta, Seattle, Denver, Nashville}.
In addition, as explained in Section 3, we also include a small number of other sport mega-events
(e.g., selected FIFA World Cup matches and Formula 1 races) to broaden the range of environmental
indicators and avoid overﬁtting to only nine observations [2, 12, 1].
To train binary classiﬁers, we deﬁne a label𝑦𝑐 ∈{ 0, 1} for each city/conﬁguration in the calibration
dataset:
𝑦𝑐 =
{

𝑐 ≤ 𝜏,

𝑐 >𝜏 ,

𝑐 : 𝑐 ∈C train}), (6)
where 𝜏 is the median equal-weight score across all cities in the training setCtrain and 𝑄0.5 denotes the
median operator. Cities in the better half of the distribution (lower 𝑆equal
𝑐 ) are labelled 𝑦𝑐 = 1 (good
host), and the rest𝑦𝑐 = 0 (average host).1
4.2 Logistic-regression model
The logistic-regression model assumes that, conditional on the environmental scores, the log-odds of
being a good host are linear ins𝑐:
log P(𝑦𝑐 = 1 | s𝑐)
P(𝑦𝑐 = 0 | s𝑐) = 𝛽0 + 𝜷⊤s𝑐, (7)
where 𝛽0 is an intercept and 𝜷 ∈ R6 is a coeﬃcient vector. Equivalently,
𝑝(LR)
𝑐 := P(𝑦𝑐 = 1 | s𝑐) = 𝜎 {𝛽0 + 𝜷⊤s𝑐
},𝜎 (𝑡) = 1
1+ 𝑒−𝑡 .
The parameters (𝛽0, 𝜷) are obtained by minimizing the regularized negative log-likelihood:
L(𝛽0, 𝜷) = −
/summationdisplay.1
𝑐∈Ctrain

𝑐 )

+𝜆‖𝜷‖2
2,
where𝜆> 0 is a small ridge penalty to avoid overﬁtting. In our implementation we ﬁt this model using
fitclinear with Learner=’logistic’ in MATLAB.
The signs and magnitudes of𝜷 provide an interpretable summary of how each dimension inﬂuences
the likelihood of being a good host (for example, negative coeﬃcients on waste and water scores mean
that cities with lower waste and water impacts are more likely to be classiﬁed as good hosts).
1This is a simple relative deﬁnition; the NFL could choose a stricter threshold (e.g., the best one-third) for more
ambitious sustainability policies.

1. February Climate LoadDeﬁned as
Ci = |72− T Feb
i |
where T Feb
i is the long–term February temperature average (NOAA climate normals). The target
of 72◦F is chosen because it approximates indoor thermal comfort.
2. Renewable Energy Share State–level renewable electricity fractions are computed from the
EIA 2024 “Annual Electric Power Industry” database. These values penalize fossil–heavy states
(e.g., TX, FL) and reward states with strong renewable portfolios (e.g., MN, MI, CA).
3. Public Transit Share Percent of commuters using public transit (ACS 5–year survey). This
lowers the travel footprint of stadiums in cities like Philadelphia or New York, but raises it for
car–dependent regions.
5.9 Final Ranking via Borda Count
To synthesize rankings across the diﬀerent weighting strategies, we apply a Borda count on the four
ranking lists: raw EF, PCA-EF, geo-adjusted EF, and geo-adjusted PCA-EF. A stadium with rankr
among n candidates receives a Borda score of (n− r+1). Summing these across all lists gives
Bi =
4∑
m=1
(n− rim +1) .
The stadium with the highest Borda score is considered the most environmentally sustainable host.
6 Model Application
6.1 Model Framework
Our environmental comparison uses a two–layer model: (1) an ecological footprint score EFi for each
stadium, and (2) a geographic adjustment parameter Gi, which accounts for regional context.

(Energy, Water, Transport, Waste)

(Equal & PCA Weights)

(Climate, Grid, Transit)

Final Consensus Ranking

Team # 16521 Page 13 of 28
6.2 Model Application to All Sixteen Stadiums
We categorize the sixteen stadiums as 13 historical Super Bowl Hosts (excluding Inglewood, Glendale,
Las Vegas, New Orleans, Santa Clara, and Atlanta) and 3 other NFL stadiums chosen randomly:
U.S. Bank Stadium, Sun Devil Stadium, Ford Field, MetLife Stadium, Hard Rock Stadium, Raymond
James Stadium, NRG Stadium, LA Memorial Coliseum, Rose Bowl, AT&T Stadium, Rice Stadium,
EverBank Stadium, Lucas Oil Stadium, Bank of America Stadium, Lincoln Financial Field, and Nissan
Stadium.
ToevaluatetheenvironmentalperformanceofpotentialSuperBowlvenues, weappliedourEcological
Footprint model to a set of sixteen stadiums: thirteen that have previously hosted the Super Bowl, and
three that have never hosted. Although individual categories (energy, water, transportation, and solid
waste) diﬀer in scale, our model compresses them into a single, comparable metric:
EFi (Ecological Footprint of stadiumi)
and modiﬁes this value by a location–speciﬁc geographic parameter:
Gi (Geographic adjustment for climate, renewables, and transit)
Producing a ﬁnal, context-aware measure:
EF ∗
i = EFi · Gi.
The result is a consistent index that captures both the raw environmental burden of operating a
Super Bowl event at stadiumi and the fairness adjustments caused by unavoidable regional diﬀerences.

6.3 Final Integrated Ranking
Table 3 summarizes the three core quantities entering our environmental evaluation: the raw ecological
footprint EF, the geographic factor G, and the PCA-based ecological footprint scoreEFPCA.E a c h
measure highlights a diﬀerent dimension of stadium performance. The rawEF captures absolute en-
vironmental burden, G reﬂects unavoidable regional conditions (climate, energy mix, and transit), and
EFPCA balances all four impact categories by removing scale bias.

6.4 Interpretation of Final Borda Rankings
The integration of EF, PCA, and geographic adjustments through the Borda framework allows us
to recognize stadiums that are environmentally competitive across multiple dimensions, rather than
excelling in only one. This consensus perspective is essential for comparing future Super Bowl sites in
a fair and transparent manner.
All sixteen were processed through the same sequence of indices: Raw EF, PCA-weighted EF, Geo-
Adjusted EF, and ﬁnally a Borda consensus ranking. This uniformity allows us to compare all cities
on equal footing. The Borda results produce a stable, intuitive ordering of environmental performance
(Table 4). U.S. Bank Stadium (Minneapolis) is the clear top performer, ranking among the top two
positions in every modeling scheme due to its eﬃcient indoor design, low climate load, and one of the
highest renewable energy fractions in the dataset.
Among stadiums that havenever hosted a Super Bowl, Lincoln Financial Field (Philadelphia) ranks
highest overall. Its strengths include strong public transit usage, a moderately renewable electricity mix,
and a highly balanced impact proﬁle that performs well under both PCA and geographic adjustments.
It consistently ranks within the upper tier of all indices, making it the most environmentally competitive
unhosted stadium.
Across all weighting strategies and across both raw and adjusted indices, U.S. Bank Stadium in
Minneapolis repeatedly emerges as the most environmentally favorable site. Three features drive its
exceptionally strong performance:
1. Low EF due to eﬃcient indoor design.The fully enclosed stadium sharply reduces climate
load in February. Heating requirements are predictable and far smaller than the cooling demands
found in southern venues.
2. Favorable geographic modiﬁers. Minnesota’s grid has one of the highest renewable energy
shares in the NFL stadium set, and Minneapolis maintains a robust public transit system. These
T e a m  #  1 6 9 4 7                                                                                    P a g e  2 1  o f  2 6  
 
The first three recommendations correspond to Sub-model 1 (carbon emissions), the fourth and 
fifth to Sub-model 2 (ecosystem impacts), and the sixth to Sub-model 3 (disturbance pollution). 
Collectively, these strategies enhance a city’s sustainability across multiple dimensions. 
   
( a )                                  ( b )  
Figure 14. Houston's rail line planning and energy structure. 
Taking Houston, ranked 10th, as an example, its current electricity energy mix primarily consists 
of natural gas, coal, and nuclear power (accounting for over 60% of total generation capacity), as 
shown in Fig 14. However, Houston holds notable  advantages in wind and solar energy, but 
substantial improvements are needed to enhance it s sustainability. We recommend significantly 
increasing financial investment over the next three years to raise the share of renewable energy to 
approximately 50%, which would reduce electricity carbon intensity from 0.45 kgCO₂/kWh to around 
0.2 kgCO₂/kWh. 
Transportation currently relies heavily on gasoli ne-powered vehicles (over 85% of trips), with 
public transit comprising only 3%. To address  this, we recommend expanding public transit 
infrastructure over the next 3–5 years, including electric buses, subways, and rail systems, alongside 
promoting the transition to electric vehicles. These measures would substantially reduce 
transportation carbon emissions and pollutant di scharges, improving both carbon emissions and air 
quality scores. 
Houston’s current recycling rate is 30%. We  advise expanding recycling infrastructure, 
increasing household recycling capacity, and improving contamination management to raise the rate 
to approximately 60%. Additiona lly, strengthening water resource and ecosystem protection, 
accelerating green ecological restoration, and promoting low-light-pollution lighting and soundproof 
building materials will reduce noise and light pollution. 
Our simulations indicate that implementing these measures could increase Houston’s overall 
environmental score from 0.2938 to 0.4250 within 3–5 years, potentially improving its ranking to 6th 
place among candidate cities. 
6.4.Sensitivity Analysis: Key Factors of Single vs Multi-Sport 
In single-sport events, carbon emissions from en ergy consumption and waste disposal are the 
most critical factors. For example, in the Supe r Bowl host city evaluation, the Los Angeles–
Inglewood–Pasadena bid outperformed San Die go on carbon emissions. Although San Diego 
demonstrated superior air quali ty and light/noise pollution cont rol, the higher weighting of carbon 
emissions allowed Los Angeles–Inglewood–Pasadena to achieve a higher overall score, as shown in 
Fig.15(a). 
In contrast, for multi-sport events, venue c onstruction impacts become paramount. In Olympic 
host city selection, Nusantara exhibited lower carbon emissions and ecological footprint than Seoul.

Based on Assumptions 1 and 2, we can conclude that the historical climatic conditions of each 
candidate city during the Super Bowl period are relatively stable, and the urban population flow pat-
terns remain consistent. Therefore, we only need to use the average values of temperature, precipitation, 
population density, and average spectator travel distance from 2015 to 2024 as proxies. For other in-
dicators such as public transportation coverage, we will predict data for the next five years based on 
historical data from 2015 to 2024.
